# Audit des nœuds « argument savant » du KG — vague 3 (2026-08-29)
Chaque nœud a été confronté au texte de l'auteur dans le fonds de la thèse (`04_Littérature_secondaire/`). Verdicts : FAITHFUL (fidèle), OVERSIMPLIFIED (perd une qualification de l'auteur), DISTORTED (contresens ou surenchère), MISATTRIBUTED (mauvais auteur/ouvrage), NOT_FOUND (source présente, énoncé introuvable), PAGE_WRONG (pages fausses), SOURCE_ABSENT (ouvrage absent du fonds).

## w3-01
Sources utilisées : 04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/01_Philosophie_antique/Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/02_Judaisme_Second_Temple/Brouwer et Vimercati - 2020 - Fate, Providence and Free Will Philosophy and Religion in Dialogue in the Early Imperial Age.md, 04_Littérature_secondaire/04_Apologistes_Justin/Justin, Philosopher and Martyr_ Apologies (Oxford Early -- Denis Minns, Paul Parvis (eds_,trans_) -- Oxford Early Christian Texts, 1, 2009 -- IRL -- 9780191570841 -- 9c6076ca2866962b6846f3a7f5b0ae13 -- Anna’s Archive (1).md · source trouvée : True
Notes de l'auditeur : Four fonds files confirmed via MANIFEST.md / page_map.json; all four works present (no SOURCE_ABSENT). Method caveats per file. (1) KOCH: the on-disk PDF (32 pp.) is a watermarked DRAFT of the Introduction with 'p. ###' placeholders, paginated 1-23 in its own footer; its body text is not extractable (the .md and a fresh pdftotext both yield ~3 400-4 000 words of footnotes, Greek and apostrophes only). agentic_search --strict-cite refuses it (AUDIT-INSUFFICIENT_SAMPLES; p.? / map_conf 0.00 for most hits). I judged the descriptions against rendered page images (pdftoppm, draft pp. 1-15) but quoted evidence only from the .md fragments. All Koch page_ranges and the verified_reference 'Classiques Garnier 2019, Introduction pp. 1-2' must be understood as draft pagination; the printed Garnier pages are [page-unverified]. Five Koch nodes ascribe to Koch positions she reports from others (Bobzien, Thillet, Duhot/Long/Inwood, Sharples) and in two cases argues against; 'Magna Moralia' (node _5) is absent from text and PDF. (2) DENZEY LEWIS: page_map is AUDIT-FAIL and systematically off by 4-7 pages (e.g. L830 mapped p.7, actual p. 11; L7808 mapped p.173, actual p. 180); all printed pages were read from in-file running heads/folios. All eight quote_verbatim strings are verbatim in the file. The epigraph page carries no folio. (3) BROUWER: extraction native, page_map good (p. 1 / p. 2 confirmed by in-file header '2 Brouwer & Vimercati' at L265); every quote verbatim; but the Introduction is signed 'René Brouwer and Emmanuele Vimercati' (L223-224) — the lot's 'Brouwer 2020' attribution should name both. (4) MINNS-PARVIS: metadata says 'native' but the text layer is OCR of two-page spreads (Latin/Greek glyph confusion, Greek without breathings/accents: [Greek-corrupt] for every Greek token in this file); printed pages derived from spread headers (190-191, 192-193, 294-295, 302-303, 54, vii). Every Minns page_range in the lot is a .md line number, not a page. The Justin Greek quoted by the lot from TLG (αὐτεξούσιον…; προαιρέσει ἐλευθέρᾳ) is citable from the project corpus at 02_Corpus/03_Apologistes.txt:275 and :208. Vocabulary: 'determinism vs. libertarianism' is Brouwer-Vimercati's own; 'libertarienne', 'compatibilisme', 'indéterministe' are Koch's own; no analytic label was imported elsewhere.

### `scholarly_argument_koch_alexander_of_aphrodisias_de_fa_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Alexander of Aphrodisias' De fato as philosophical work — Acknowledges that Alexander's De fato is philosophically less successful than other works, following assessments by Duhot and Long, but still worthy of serious study
- Constat : Koch (draft p. 1) reports how the De fato 'est perçu' — as 'une œuvre philosophiquement moins réussie' than Alexander's other treatises and commentaries — citing four critics in n. 5 (Donini 1974, Thillet 1984, Duhot 1989, Long 1970), not only Duhot and Long. Her own framing is the treatise's 'statut paradoxal': on draft p. 4 it is nonetheless Alexander's most translated and most studied treatise, a major document for ancient debates on determinism. The node turns a reported reception into Koch's own 'acknowledgment' and flattens the paradox into 'still worthy of serious study'. The .md holds only footnote fragments (body text not extractable from the PDF); body text checked on rendered page images of the on-disk DRAFT.
  - « œuvre philosophiquement moins réussie » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:51]
  - « « sʼen tient à un plan très superficiel qui ne résiste pas à lʼanalyse » (Duhot, repère un certain nombre de pétitions de principe, dʼarguments qui ratent leur cible ou ne font que lʼeffleurer, de réfutations obscures, etc. (Long, 1970, p. 250, 251, 252, 257, » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:85-88]
- Description proposée : Koch opens on the De fato's 'statut paradoxal': critics (Donini, Thillet, Duhot, Long) perceive it as 'une œuvre philosophiquement moins réussie' than Alexander's other treatises and Aristotle commentaries, with incoherences and doctrinal approximations that feed doubts about the adversaries' identity; yet it is paradoxically his most translated and most studied work, a major document for ancient debates on determinism (logical, modal, physical, ethico-practical), which since the 1970s have most interested readers of ancient texts.
- Pages : draft pp. 1, 4 (on-disk DRAFT pagination; Classiques Garnier printed pages [page-unverified: Koch 2019, Introduction])

### `scholarly_argument_koch_alexander_s_aristotelian_sourc_5` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Alexander's Aristotelian sources and authenticity — Alexander draws on Aristotelian discussions of τὸ ἐφ’ ἡμῖν and ἐνδεχόμενον, but the authenticity of some texts he uses (like the Magna Moralia and possibly Theophrastus's On Callisthenes) is disputed; Alexander's own De fato shows tensions with his other works on the analysis of contingency
- Constat : The Magna Moralia is nowhere in the file or in the PDF (grep 'magna|grandes morales': 0 hits). What Koch says (draft p. 3) is that Aristotle elaborated no doctrine of fate and never uses εἱμαρμένη except in a Poetics citation; the attribution rests on Mantissa XXV — a collection 'dont lʼauthenticité est discutée au cas par cas' (n. 5) — and a thin Peripatetic tradition: Polyzelos' On Fate and Theophrastus' Callisthenes, a lost work, probably a consolatio (n. 6). The 'authenticity dispute' concerns the Mantissa, not the Callisthenes. On draft p. 7 (nn. 4-5) she notes the ambiguity of Aristotle's analysis of the contingent (capacity for contraries readable indeterministically or compatibly with determinism) and that the Mantissa text central to Bobzien's reading may not be by Alexander — not 'tensions between De fato and his other works'.
  - « ensemble de textes dont lʼauthenticité est discutée au cas par cas, » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:239]
  - « 31. Lʼouvrage attribué à Théophraste est perdu, mais il catalogue des œuvres de Théophraste Il sʼagissait probablement dʼun texte composé à la mort de Callisthène dʼOlynthe, jeune parent dʼAristote » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:243-246]
  - « comprise soit en un sens indéterministe, soit en un sens compatible avec le déterminisme, selon quʼon inclut ou non dans lʼanalyse la prise en compte des circonstances » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:473-474]
- Description proposée : Aristotle elaborated no doctrine of fate; the attribution of one rests on Mantissa XXV (a collection whose authenticity is discussed case by case) and a thin, hard-to-reconstruct Peripatetic tradition (Polyzelos' On Fate; Theophrastus' lost Callisthenes, probably a consolatio). Koch adds (draft p. 7, nn. 4-5) that Aristotle's analysis of the contingent as a capacity for contraries can be read either indeterministically or compatibly with determinism depending on whether circumstances are included, and that the Mantissa passage central to Bobzien's indeterminist reading is not certainly by Alexander.
- Pages : draft pp. 3-4, 7 (on-disk DRAFT pagination; printed pages [page-unverified: Koch 2019, Introduction])

### `scholarly_argument_koch_alexander_s_conception_of_what_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Alexander's conception of τὸ ἐφ’ ἡμῖν (what depends on us) — Alexander defines τὸ ἐφ’ ἡμῖν through the power to choose opposites (δύναμις τῶν ἐναντίων), an indeterminist conception that requires the ability to do otherwise under the same circumstances; this is fundamentally different from Stoic compatibilist accounts
- Constat : Draft p. 6 presents the 'absolute'/categorical reading of the two-way power as Bobzien's ('dʼaprès Suzanne Bobzien'), built on the Mantissa sentence quoted in n. 2 (Bruns 174,10). Draft p. 7 immediately qualifies: the indeterminist interpretation 'ne fait pas elle-même lʼunanimité'; readers note the 'pouvoir agir autrement' remains ambiguous or hypothetical; D. Frede reduces Alexander and the Stoics to 'deux variantes de compatibilisme' (pp. 7-8); Koch herself (pp. 8-10) refuses to make such localisation her aim. The node attributes Bobzien's contested reading to Koch as Alexander's settled 'definition', and the clause 'fundamentally different from Stoic compatibilist accounts' is precisely what the Frede reading she reports denies. Note the Mantissa (not De fato ch. 8) is the source of the quoted sentence.
  - « ἔχων ἐν αὐτῷ τὴν ἐξουσίαν τοῦ βουλεύεσθαι περὶ τῶν περιεστώτων, ἔχει καὶ τὸ δύνασθαι ἐκ τῶν αὐτῶν μὴ τὰ αὐτὰ αἱρεῖσθαι. La phrase est dʼAlexandre (Bobzien, 1998b, p. 400). » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:418-420]
  - « In these accounts phrases of the kind “having the power to do/choose opposites” » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:414]
  - « » Même appréciation dʼune doctrine inaboutie chez Bobzien, » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:463]
- Description proposée : Koch reports Bobzien's reading: Alexander's clarity lies in founding responsibility not merely on an equal power to act or not, but on interpreting that double possibility as absolute — 'ἐκ τῶν αὐτῶν μὴ τὰ αὐτὰ αἱρεῖσθαι' (Mantissa, Bruns 174,10) — a categorical power to do otherwise, not a counterfactual one. She stresses that this reading is not unanimous: for some the formulations remain ambiguous or hypothetical, Alexander's doctrine unfinished (Frede M., Bobzien), and for D. Frede Alexander and the Stoics amount to two variants of compatibilism. Koch declines to settle this localisation.
- Pages : draft pp. 6-8 (on-disk DRAFT pagination; printed pages [page-unverified: Koch 2019, Introduction])

### `scholarly_argument_koch_alexander_s_rhetorical_strateg_6` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Alexander's rhetorical strategy and political context — Alexander's anonymity in attacking contemporary Stoics reflects political caution due to his recent appointment to a Peripatetic chair (198-209 CE under Septimius Severus and Caracalla); he names dead philosophers but anonymizes living contemporaries, though this anonymity is only one reason among others for his approach
- Constat : The political explanation is Thillet's, reported in n. 1 of draft p. 10 (taken up by Labarrière 2014, p. 137): the book was offered to the Emperors, and Septimius Severus, wishing like Pertinax to emulate Marcus Aurelius, would not have welcomed a nominal attack on Stoic philosophy — the reason is the imperial dedication, not Alexander's 'recent appointment'. Koch's own qualification runs the other way from the node: because anonymity of interlocutors is his practice elsewhere, 'le statut particulier du De fato ne peut toutefois en constituer quʼune raison parmi dʼautres' — the special status is one reason among others for the anonymity, not 'anonymity is one reason among others for his approach'. Draft p. 14 gives the practice: he does not name contemporaries but names long-dead philosophers. Dating 198-209 from the dedication is on draft p. 1 n. 3.
  - « politique de lʼanonymat des cibles volonté de sʼinspirer, à lʼinstar de son prédécesseur , il aurait été malvenu dʼattaquer , 2014, p. 137). Comme lʼanonymat des interlocut toutefois en constituer quʼune raison parmi dʼautres. » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:650-655]
  - « pas citer le nom de ses contemporains, alors quʼil nominative les philosophes morts depuis longtemps lorsquʼil d » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:935-936]
  - « μέγιστοι αὐτοκράτορες Σεβῆρε καὶἈντωνῖνε, 164, 3. Il sʼagit donc de Septime Sévère et de lʼouvrage entre 198 et 209 ap. J. » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:71-73]
- Description proposée : Koch reports Thillet's political explanation of the De fato's anonymous targets: a book offered to the Emperors, when Septimius Severus wished, like Pertinax, to emulate Marcus Aurelius, could hardly attack Stoic philosophy by name (Thillet 1984; Labarrière 2014). Since anonymity of interlocutors is Alexander's practice elsewhere, she holds that the De fato's particular status can only be one reason among others. Alexander tends not to name contemporaries while naming long-dead philosophers. The dedication to Severus and Antoninus dates the treatise between 198 and 209.
- Pages : draft pp. 1, 10, 13-14 (on-disk DRAFT pagination; printed pages [page-unverified: Koch 2019, Introduction])

### `scholarly_argument_koch_alexander_s_target_stoic_deter_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Alexander's target: Stoic determinism vs. specific Stoic philosophers — Alexander's critique is directed against a reconstructed and simplified Stoic doctrine rather than accurately reporting specific Stoic philosophers; he deliberately distorts Stoic positions and maintains anonymity of contemporary targets for political reasons
- Constat : The 'reconstructed and simplified doctrine' and 'deliberate distortion' theses are those of Gercke, Long and Duhot (draft p. 2 n. 1) and Inwood (draft p. 4 n. 3), which Koch reports. Her own move (draft pp. 13-14) is to offer 'une lecture alternative plus riche' against reading the De fato's doctrinal peculiarities as 'déformations maladroites ou, pire, malintentionnées': the anonymous interlocutors are imperial-era Stoics whose school formulations had evolved over five centuries. The node thus ascribes to Koch the very reading she argues against. What she does affirm (draft p. 10) is that Stoic determinism is 'hors de doute' the principal target. Political anonymity = Thillet's hypothesis, one reason among others (see node _6).
  - « », et sʼattaque ainsi à une doctrine reconstruite et simplifiée qui nʼest pour lui « ʼ » Duhot, 1989 » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:136-139]
  - « « It is not difficult for Alexander to ridicule this notion of free action. […] This is not a reliable report, for there is no distinction between man and beast in the “Stoic” doctrine according to Alexander. […] In Alexanderʼs case at least, the distortion seems to have been deliberate. » » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:301-303]
  - « Si la mention de contemporains est assez rare, cependant, lorsquʼAlexandre , il est probable quʼil y englobe aussi ceux qui professent leurs lʼépoque. » » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:918-920]
- Description proposée : Koch reports, without endorsing, the verdict of Gercke, Long, Duhot and Inwood that Alexander attacks 'une doctrine reconstruite et simplifiée', an adversary made to measure, with distortions that seem deliberate. Against reading the De fato's doctrinal peculiarities as clumsy or malicious deformations, she proposes a richer alternative: Alexander's anonymous interlocutors are imperial-era Stoics faithful to a Chrysippean orthodoxy whose formulations had evolved after five centuries of inter-school competition — while granting that Stoic determinism is beyond doubt the treatise's principal target.
- Pages : draft pp. 2, 4, 10, 13-14 (on-disk DRAFT pagination; printed pages [page-unverified: Koch 2019, Introduction])

### `scholarly_argument_koch_necessity_and_fate_alexander_s_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Necessity and fate: Alexander's critique of Stoic distinction — Alexander reports that Stoics distinguish fate from necessity (φασιν μηδὲ τὰ γιγνόμενα καθ’ εἱμαρμένην καίτοι ἀπαραβάτως γινόμενα ἐξ ἀνάγκης γίνεσθαι), but argues this distinction fails; he attributes to them a necessity that is not from force (οὐ τὴν ἐκ βίας) but from the impossibility of things happening otherwise given their nature
- Constat : Draft pp. 11-12: Koch's point is that Alexander often treats fate and necessity as equivalents, even in a single formula, yet 'se montre tout à fait conscient' that the Stoics seek to distance themselves from pure necessitarianism, though in his eyes without adequate means (Cicero likewise reports Chrysippus' distinction). N. 2 (p. 12) quotes De fato 10 (the Stoics say this 'pour sauvegarder la notion de δυνατόν'); n. 3 attributes the two forms of necessity, only one ἐκ βίας, to the 'tout destin' partisans in De fato 13. The Greek in the node is verbatim in the .md (L779-780, L788). The node hardens 'without adequate means' into 'argues this distinction fails' and drops Alexander's own conflation of the two terms.
  - « disent quʼil ne se trouve pas que les choses qui arrivent selon le destin, quoiquʼelles se produisent dʼune manière inviolable, se produisent par nécessité », φασιν μηδὲ τὰ γιγνόμενα καθʼ εἱμαρμένην καίτοι ἀπαραβάτως γινόμενα ἐξ ἀνάγκης γίνεσθαι. Ici encore, Alexandre se montre tout à » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:778-780]
  - « 3 La distinction entre deux formes de nécessité, dont lʼune seulement est ἐκ βίας, est attribuée par Alexandre aux partisans du “tout destin” dans un exposé de leur conception du τὸ ἐφʼἡμῖν » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:782-783]
  - « sité qui découle, non dʼune contrainte (κατʼ ἀνάγκην οὐ τὴν ἐκ βίας) » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:788]
- Description proposée : Koch observes that Alexander often uses fate and necessity as equivalents, sometimes in one formula, yet shows himself fully aware — as Cicero does for Chrysippus — that the Stoics seek to distinguish themselves from pure necessitarianism, though in his eyes without adequate means: De fato 10 has them say that what happens by fate, though inviolably, does not happen by necessity, so as to safeguard the δυνατόν; De fato 13 attributes to them a necessity 'οὐ τὴν ἐκ βίας' but from a thing's nature being unable to move otherwise.
- Pages : draft pp. 11-12 (on-disk DRAFT pagination; printed pages [page-unverified: Koch 2019, Introduction])

### `scholarly_argument_koch_the_human_problem_of_free_will_4` — **DISTORTED** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : The 'human problem' of free will and determinism — The problem of free will versus determinism is genuinely a human problem that arises from our experience of deliberation and choice; Alexander's work addresses this experiential dimension
- Constat : No passage says the free-will problem is 'genuinely a human problem arising from our experience of deliberation and choice'. The only 'problème du libre arbitre et du déterminisme' on draft p. 5 is Koch's French quotation of Sharples' 1983 introduction (n. 2 notes that Sharples' original spoke of 'responsibility and determinism'); Koch's framing is Sharples' concern to feed contemporary debates through dialogue with ancient philosophy. Draft p. 7 concerns the disputed indeterminist reading: n. 3 quotes Frede (M.) 2011, p. 100 on Alexander's failure to give a coherent account of how such a free will is possible, 'même appréciation dʼune doctrine inaboutie chez Bobzien'. De interpretatione 9 is not on these pages. The 'experiential dimension' thesis is the node's invention.
  - « problème du libre arbitre et du déterminisme ‒ un problème qui es » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:329]
  - « (M.), 2011, p. 100 : « » Même appréciation dʼune doctrine inaboutie chez Bobzien, » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:462-463]
  - « 2008b, p. 286 287. » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:348]
- Description proposée : Koch situates the De fato's modern fortune in Sharples' concern to nourish contemporary philosophical debates by dialogue with ancient philosophy, quoting his 1983 judgment that the treatise is perhaps antiquity's most exhaustive surviving treatment of 'le problème du libre arbitre et du déterminisme', still philosophically pertinent (Sharples' original wording: 'responsibility and determinism'). She notes M. Frede's and Bobzien's shared assessment of an unfinished doctrine: Alexander introduces a 'decision-making faculty' without a coherent account of how such a free will is possible.
- Pages : draft pp. 5, 7 (on-disk DRAFT pagination; printed pages [page-unverified: Koch 2019, Introduction])

### `scholarly_argument_koch_2019_freewill_grid_reservations` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Koch declines to make the free-will-problem questionnaire her own frame for reading Alexander. Three reservations: (1) the battery of categories (compatibilism, incompatibilism, non-predeterminism, soft and hard determinism, libertarianism...) insidiously fixes reflection on a game of localisation — placing each author in the right box and endlessly refining new boxes; (2) once a criterion is publicly recognised as pertinent (e.g. categorical vs hypothetical possibility in Alexander's 'power to do otherwise'), commentators read texts like a physician scrutinising a patient for THE symptom, ami
- Constat : Matches draft pp. 8-10 closely: 'Mes réticences à lʼégard de cette grille de lecture sont au nombre de trois' (localisation game and ever-new boxes; the physician scrutinising the patient for THE symptom amid a confused clinical picture, with the categorical/hypothetical possibility criterion as example; the illusion of a mature age putting order into ancient exchanges, the 'lunettes' image), then 'ce recours doit être limité, pratiqué avec distance, et surtout quʼil ne doit jamais constituer une finalité, mais seulement un instrument', and the sentence that reading the De fato does not aim to establish whether Alexander 'défendait déjà, ou presque, ou pas tout à fait, ou pas du tout' an indeterminist conception of human causality (p. 10). The Bobzien 1998a, p. 137 objection is n. 5 of draft p. 7. The node correctly labels the pagination as the on-disk draft's. Only fragments survive in the .md; body text verified on rendered page images.
  - « ἐφʼἡμῖν by “free will” or cognates of “free” » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:541-542]
  - « ʼœil nu, évidence qui nʼ » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:588]
  - « : il nʼest donc pas sûr que lʼauteur en soit Alexandre. » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:484-485]

### `scholarly_argument_koch_2019_imperial_stoic_interlocutors` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance medium)
- Nœud actuel : Stoic determinism is indubitably the De fato's principal target, but Alexander names no contemporary: Zeno and Cleanthes never appear in the corpus, Chrysippus nine times, and the usual formula is hoi apo tēs Stoas. Since the difficulties of the Stoic doctrine of fate were debated for five centuries, his interlocutors are most probably imperial-era authors defending a Chrysippan orthodoxy — Theiler and Bobzien identify behind chapters 13-14 (circumstances + proper nature determining necessarily, absent obstacle) a later Stoic theory, possibly Philopator's. This hypothesis grounds a richer alte
- Constat : Matches draft pp. 10 and 13-15: Stoic determinism 'hors de doute' the principal target (p. 10); neither Zeno nor Cleanthes cited (all Ζήνων occurrences = Zeno of Elea), Chrysippus nine times, οἱ ἀπὸ τῆς Στοᾶς preferred (p. 13); five centuries of debate, imperial-era interlocutors defending Chrysippean orthodoxy; Theiler and Bobzien on chs. 13-14 (circumstances + proper nature + absence of obstacle), possibly Philopator per Nemesius (p. 14 n. 1); the 'lecture alternative plus riche' to 'déformations maladroites ou, pire, malintentionnées' (p. 14); Megarians in ch. 9 (Donini), Galen and Atticus, Rashed on intra-Peripatetic debate as 'le plus important combat' (p. 15 n. 3), school-fictions built from aporiai (Donini 1977, Sharples 1975a). Body text verified on rendered page images; .md holds fragments only.
  - « la relative certitude sur lʼaffiliation scolaire des thèses visées ne sʼaccompagne pas dʼune identification plus précise Stoïcisme, lorsquʼil le fait cʼest en préférant la formule générale οἱἀπὸ τῆς Στοᾶς » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:770-772]
  - « 1 Dʼaprès le témoignage de Némésius, il pourrait sʼagir de Philopator, siècle (dʼaprès le ), auteur dʼun traité , 1998b, p. 368 » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:967-971]
  - « 6 Cʼest notamment lʼhypothèse de Donini » [La_Causalite_humaine_Sur_le_De_fato_dAle.md:983]

### `scholarly_argument_denzey_lewis_astral_determinism_in_gospel_o_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Astral determinism in Gospel of Judas — The Gospel of Judas exhibits astral 'determinism' rooted in Jewish apocalyptic traditions rather than heimarmene proper; the language of stars and sidereal enslavement saturates the text but represents a different conceptual framework from other Nag Hammadi texts
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed verbatim at L830-836 (printed p. 11, Introduction; header 'introduction 11' at L822-823). The node loses two qualifications: (a) Denzey Lewis says the GosJud's 'rhetorical deployment of the language of sidereal enslavement seems to me identical with other second- and third-century texts' (L840-841) — the difference is the conceptual source, not the rhetoric; (b) she stresses it 'offers no system of salvation or way out' (L838-839; p. 180: 'no salvation, no providential intervention, and no way out'). 'Different conceptual framework from other Nag Hammadi texts' is imprecise: GosJud is a Codex Tchacos text, and she contrasts it with 'the other texts I have discussed in this book' (L7177). The absence of the word heimarmene is explicit at p. 165 (L7180).
  - « the GosJud’s rhetorical deployment of the language of sidereal enslavement seems to me identical with other second- and third-century texts; its author clearly positions himself outside or apart from those whom he perceives as enslaved to a demonic cosmos. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:840-843]
  - « heimarmene. Indeed, the word heimarmene is not to be found in the text as it exists, and I doubt that when the document is fully restored, it will be found there. The concept of Providence or Pronoia is likewise absent. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:7180-7182]
  - « the GosJud alone offers no salvation, no providential intervention, and no way out. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:7808-7809]
- Description proposée : The Gospel of Judas invokes the language of astral fatalism frequently, but its discourse of astral enslavement nowhere draws on contemporaneous Graeco-Roman cosmology or philosophical physics; its author draws on older Jewish apocalyptic traditions equating the stars with the priests administering the Jerusalem Temple. The word heimarmene and the concept of Pronoia are absent, and the text — unlike the others discussed in the book — offers no system of salvation or 'way out'. Yet its rhetorical deployment of sidereal enslavement is identical with other second- and third-century texts.
- Pages : 11; 165-180 (esp. 165, 180)

### `scholarly_argument_denzey_lewis_baptism_as_cosmic_freedom_and__6` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Baptism as cosmic freedom and new genesis — Baptism functions as a ritual mechanism for achieving cosmic freedom, representing a 'new genesis' that liberates from astral and fatal enslavement; this ritual dimension is essential for understanding how ancient practitioners conceptualized escape from fate
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed verbatim at L7141-7150 (printed p. 163, ch. 7 conclusion; header '163' at L7137). The node generalises to 'ancient practitioners' and 'astral and fatal enslavement' where Denzey Lewis speaks of three Christians (Theodotus, Justin Martyr, Tatian) and of removal 'from a life in which he or she was blindly enslaved to sin' into 'moral freedom'; it drops her Introduction point (p. 11) that 'genesis' can also be translated 'horoscope' and that the same tropes occur in Justin and the Valentinian Theodotus, so that the Gnostic/proto-orthodox division 'fails us'. 'Essential for understanding how ancient practitioners conceptualized escape from fate' is the node's gloss.
  - « Christians of the second century universally agreed that baptism, as an event, had resounding cosmological ramifications. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:7150-7152]
  - « we find in the rhetoric of Justin Martyr, for example, an identical set of tropes to what we find in the excerpts of the Valentinian teacherTheodotus:thatChristianswere‘enslavedtofate’onlyuntilbaptism, at which point they were granted a new birth or ‘genesis,’ a word that also can be translated as ‘horoscope.’ » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:825-829]
- Description proposée : For Theodotus, Justin Martyr and Tatian, baptism provided a deeply spiritual experience — 'illumination', a spiritual transport out of the cosmos onto a new plane — amounting to a new birth, a new 'genesis' (a word also translatable as 'horoscope'), removing the individual from blind enslavement to sin into moral freedom. Second-century Christians, 'Gnostic' and 'proto-orthodox' alike, agreed that baptism had resounding cosmological ramifications: they were 'enslaved to fate' only until baptism.
- Pages : 11; 145-164 (quotation p. 163)

### `scholarly_argument_denzey_lewis_gnostic_cosmic_pessimism_as_sc_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gnostic cosmic pessimism as scholarly construct — The category of 'cosmic pessimism' as applied to Gnostics is a constructed worldview that reflects classicists' biases toward normative canons rather than actual ancient diversity; Bob Lamberton's work helped reveal this as limited
- Constat : Two passages fused, one mis-paged. The Lamberton credit is on p. ix (Acknowledgements, L124-127): he raised 'the specter of cosmic pessimism as a wholly constructed worldview' AND set aside 'the conventional classicist's biases as limited to a more normative canon of texts and approaches' — two distinct debts, which the node welds into a causal claim (the category 'reflects classicists' biases'). The quote_verbatim is not on pp. ix-x but at L1407-1414 = printed p. 26 (header '26 chapter one' at L1391-1392), the conclusion of ch. 1, whose actual thesis — that the tropes characterize a movement that 'was not a true movement at all, but a historiographical invention', hence the terminology 'Gnosticism'/'Gnostic' should be dropped — is missing from the description.
  - « raising the specter of ‘cosmic pessimism’ as a wholly constructed worldview, and by setting aside the conventional classicist’s biases as limited to a more normative canon of texts and approaches. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:125-127]
  - « my insistence that we disentangle the language or the presumed attitude of cosmic fatalism and pessimism from what people have, in my opinion, been too quick to call Gnosticism. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:1405-1407]
  - « to characterize the spiritual tenor of a movement that was not a true movement at all, but a historiographical invention. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:1410-1411]
- Description proposée : Denzey Lewis credits Bob Lamberton with setting her on the path to the book by raising the specter of 'cosmic pessimism' as a wholly constructed worldview, and by setting aside the conventional classicist's biases limited to a normative canon of texts (p. ix). Chapter 1 argues that key modern scholarship invokes the tropes of 'cosmic pessimism' and 'enslavement to fate' to characterize a movement that was 'a historiographical invention'; she proposes rejecting these characterizations along with an unconsidered use of 'Gnosticism' and 'Gnostic' (p. 26).
- Pages : ix; 13-27 (quotation p. 26)

### `scholarly_argument_denzey_lewis_heimarmene_fate_in_nag_hammadi_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Heimarmene (fate) in Nag Hammadi texts — Heimarmene appears as a technical term and conceptual category in specific Nag Hammadi texts (Apocryphon of John, On the Origin of the World) but not in others like the Gospel of Judas, which draws on different conceptual materials
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed verbatim at L3994-4001, printed p. 86 (header '86 chapter four' at L3977-3978). The verified_reference is wrong on structure: the passage belongs to ch. 4, 'Heimarmene at Nag Hammadi: The Apocryphon of John and On the Origin of the World' (pp. 85-102 per TOC), not 'chs. 2 and 5'. The description drops her count and typology: heimarmene occurs in only six of the fifty-two tractates (L3986-3988) — Disc. 8-9; Eugnostos and SJC, which reject it; Tri.Prot., ApJn and Orig.Wld, which adopt it as a causal principle with conflicting notions. The GosJud contrast is right (p. 165) but GosJud is not a Nag Hammadi text.
  - « The word heimarmene, specifically, is mentioned in only six of the Nag Hammadi library’s fifty-two tractates. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:3986-3988]
  - « All three authors adopted heimarmene as a causal principle within the cosmic oikonomia; yet they disagreed on both the precise effects and the ultimate consequences of fate’s influence. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:3999-4002]
- Description proposée : Heimarmene is mentioned in only six of the fifty-two Nag Hammadi tractates: the Hermetic Discourse on the Eighth and Ninth; Eugnostos and the Sophia of Jesus Christ, which explicitly reject it as a source of being or influence; and the Trimorphic Protennoia, Apocryphon of John and On the Origin of the World, which adopt it as a causal principle within the cosmic oikonomia while disagreeing on its effects and consequences (ch. 4). The Gospel of Judas (Codex Tchacos) lacks the word altogether, as it lacks Pronoia (p. 165).
- Pages : 85-102 (quotation p. 86); 165

### `scholarly_argument_denzey_lewis_lucretian_critique_of_divine_d_7` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Lucretian critique of divine design and cosmic fault — The epigraph from Lucretius (De rerum natura 5.198-199) frames the book's thematic concern: the frailty and faultiness of a world that seems not divinely designed, resonating with Gnostic and Graeco-Roman explorations of cosmic imperfection and the problem of evil
- Constat : The epigraph and its English rendering are in the file verbatim (L69-73), on the dedication page ('For my girls, Lola and Isobel'), which carries no printed folio; it precedes the Contents (p. ix), so 'vii' cannot be confirmed from the file. Denzey Lewis makes no statement about the epigraph anywhere; the claim that it 'frames the book's thematic concern' and 'resonat[es] with Gnostic and Graeco-Roman explorations of cosmic imperfection and the problem of evil' is the node's interpretation presented as a 'modern_scholarly_position'. Nothing in the file supports treating an epigraph as an argument.
  - « Nequaquam nobis divinitus esse paratam. Naturam rerum; tanta stat praedita culpa. “Had God designed the world, it would not be A world so frail and faulty as we see.” —Lucretius, De rerum natura, 5.198–199 » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:69-73]
- Description proposée : The book's dedication page carries, without comment, the epigraph Lucretius, De rerum natura 5.198-199 ('Nequaquam nobis divinitus esse paratam. Naturam rerum; tanta stat praedita culpa') with the English rendering 'Had God designed the world, it would not be / A world so frail and faulty as we see.' Denzey Lewis nowhere discusses it; any 'framing' function is the reader's inference, not a position stated by the author.
- Pages : [page-unverified: unnumbered dedication page, front matter before Contents p. ix]

### `scholarly_argument_denzey_lewis_middle_platonism_heimarmene_an_4` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Middle Platonism, heimarmene, and Corpus Hermeticum — Middle Platonic traditions and the Corpus Hermeticum offer important comparative material for understanding how heimarmene was conceptualized in Graeco-Roman religious philosophy; these texts provide a wider palette for analyzing fate and cosmic order
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed verbatim at L5585-5596 = printed p. 125 (running head 'middle platonism, heimarmene, and the corpus hermeticum 125' at L5576), the Conclusions of ch. 5 (pp. 103-126). The description replaces her substantive claims with generic 'comparative material'/'wider palette' language (the 'wider palette' phrase is borrowed from the Acknowledgements about Fowden, p. ix, L129-130). Lost: Middle Platonists moved away from the integrative Stoic conception, so one could theoretically stand outside heimarmene; Hermetists and Middle Platonists delineated heimarmene from necessity and providence partly to save providence from evil; some Hermetic texts identified heimarmene with specific celestial bodies.
  - « the Middle Platonists that provided the chief intellectual inspiration for the Hermetists had moved away from a more integrative Stoic conception of heimarmene as a universal principle that worked through all things; it was theoretically possible, they argued, to stand outside its influence. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:5587-5591]
  - « this was partly an attempt to save the principle of divine providence from the reality of misfortune and evil, which could be attributed to the lesser principle of fate. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:5593-5596]
- Description proposée : In the period when the Hermetic writings were composed, the Middle Platonists who chiefly inspired the Hermetists had moved away from the more integrative Stoic conception of heimarmene as a universal principle working through all things: it was theoretically possible to stand outside its influence. Hermetists, like Middle Platonists, actively debated how to delineate heimarmene from necessity and providence — partly to save divine providence from misfortune and evil, attributable to the lesser principle of fate; some Hermetic texts identified heimarmene with specific celestial bodies (planetary, astral or zodiacal).
- Pages : 103-126 (quotation p. 125)

### `scholarly_argument_denzey_lewis_pauline_exegesis_and_cosmic_en_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Pauline exegesis and cosmic enslavement to sin — Pauline concept of 'body of death' represents cosmic malevolence and enslavement to sin that intersects with Gnostic and Graeco-Roman fate discourses; this chapter examines how Pauline exegesis contributes to understanding cosmic bondage
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed verbatim at L2635-2639 = printed p. 53 (opening page of ch. 3; header '54 chapter three' at L2642-2643). The description misplaces the claim: Denzey Lewis's assertion — flagged as diverging 'from the majority of scholars' — is that second- to fourth-century cosmic pessimism is rooted 'not just' in Graeco-Roman conceptions of a malevolent cosmos 'but also in later exegeses of the Pauline corpus'. The node speaks vaguely of a Pauline concept that 'intersects with' fate discourses and of 'how Pauline exegesis contributes to understanding cosmic bondage', losing both the divergence and the genealogical claim (later exegesis of Paul as a root).
  - « It is my assertion—and here I diverge from the majority of scholars on the subject—that cosmic pessimism within second to fourth-century Chris- tianity (particularly, though by no means exclusively, ‘Gnostic’ Christianity) finds its root not just in prevailing Graeco-Roman conceptions of a malevo- lent cosmos, but also in later exegeses of the Pauline corpus. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:2635-2639]
  - « Gnostic authors, in particular, had such a profound respect and fascination for the Pauline corpus that Paul’s sta- tus as an apostle within orthodox circles teetered perilously close to the abyss for a century or so. » [Nicola Denzey Lewis - Cosmology and Fate in Gnosticism and Graeco-Roman Antiquity_ Under Pitiless Skies (2013, BRILL) - libgen.lc.md:2645-2648]
- Description proposée : Diverging from the majority of scholars, Denzey Lewis asserts that cosmic pessimism in second- to fourth-century Christianity (particularly, though not exclusively, 'Gnostic' Christianity) finds its root not only in prevailing Graeco-Roman conceptions of a malevolent cosmos but also in later exegeses of the Pauline corpus: a Christian wishing to develop the idea of an enslaving cosmos 'needed to look no farther than Paul', whom Gnostic authors held in such respect that his apostolic status in orthodox circles was imperilled (Tertullian's 'apostolos haereticorum').
- Pages : 53-84 (quotation p. 53)

- Nœud actuel : Savior god interventions as liberation from fate — Interventions of a savior god represent one 'way out' of cosmic fate and enslavement; this soteriological pattern appears across Gnostic and related texts as a means of transcending heimarmene
- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed verbatim at L6369-6374 = printed p. 144 (header '144 chapter six' at L6347-6348), closing ch. 6 'Ways Out I: Interventions of the Savior God' (pp. 127-144). - Constat : Located at L259-272: Gregory of Nyssa sentence begins on p. 1 (L259-261), Augustine, Boethius and the Consolation on p. 2 (L267-271, after the header '2 Brouwer & Vimercati' at L265). In late antiquity, the discussion was continued by the Greek and Latin church fathers, among whom can be singled out Gregory of Nyssa (c. 335–395), especially in his Against » [Brouwer et Vimercati - 2020 - Fate, Providence and Free Will Philosophy and Religion in Dialogue in the Early Imperial Age.md:259-261]

- Constat : quote_verbatim confirmed verbatim at L232-243, printed p. 1. The 'rethought their doctrines using philosophical terminology related to the emerging notion of free will' clause comes from the following paragraph (L256-258), also p. 1. Co-authored by Brouwer and Vimercati (L223-224).

### `scholarly_argument_donini_aristotle_s_consistency_on_det_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle's consistency on determinism and related issues — Aristotle held a consistent theory or set of theories about determinism, the voluntary, and related matters throughout his life; many misunderstandings have obscured this consistency
- Constat : The claim rests on Huby's review, not on Donini's own words. Huby hedges ('his central theme is not entirely clear, but he seems to believe…') and immediately objects that a consistent and plausible theory is 'unlikely'. The node states the thesis flatly as Donini's and drops both the hedge and the reviewer's dissent. Printed p. 370 is legible in the file (running head '370 THE CLASSICAL REVIEW', line 14) but agentic_search refuses the file (AUDIT-INSUFFICIENT_SAMPLES, map_conf 0.50, page_map gives 'p.1'), so page left empty. The 2010 English translation of Ethos in the fonds contains no programmatic statement of this thesis (grep 'consisten' hits only local claims, e.g. the theory of truth, line 85).
  - « Because he - rightly - discusses much on the level of detail, his central theme is not entirely clear, but he seems to believe that Aristotle held a consistent theory, or set of theories, about these matters throughout his life. » [aristotle-and-determinism-pier-luigi-donini-ethos-aristotele-e-il-determinismo-culture-antiche-studi-e-testi-2-pp-vi154-alessandria-edizioni-dellorso-1989-paper-l-30000.md:28-30]
  - « in general consistent theories are implausible, and plausible ones inconsistent upon close examination. Hence it is unlikely that Aristotle held a consistent and plausible theory. » [aristotle-and-determinism-pier-luigi-donini-ethos-aristotele-e-il-determinismo-culture-antiche-studi-e-testi-2-pp-vi154-alessandria-edizioni-dellorso-1989-paper-l-30000.md:51-53]
- Description proposée : As reported by the reviewer P. M. Huby (CR 41.2, 1991, p. 370), Donini 'seems to believe that Aristotle held a consistent theory, or set of theories, about these matters throughout his life', and tries to show that 'many misunderstandings', once corrected, let us see Aristotle's thought clearly. Huby adds that 'his central theme is not entirely clear' and herself objects that 'it is unlikely that Aristotle held a consistent and plausible theory'. The fonds holds only Huby's review and the 2010 English translation (chs. I-V), not Donini's own statement of this thesis.

### `scholarly_argument_donini_cicero_s_de_fato_and_aristotle_5` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Cicero's De Fato and Aristotle as determinist — Cicero's claim that Aristotle was in a certain sense a determinist should be traced to Carneades via Clitomachus as source, not Antiochus; possibly Cicero added Aristotle's name independently; Aristotle is placed in the determinist group because Cicero thinks that in his view the movements of the soul are caused by external forces amounting to Fate; external circumstances and natural endowments affect men's behavior, and the Magna Moralia's statement that nature (phusis) is needed for a man to be good may have been the line of thought
- Constat : Huby's report of Donini's final chapter (p. 371) matches the description point by point (Carneades via Clitomachus rather than Antiochus; possible independent addition of Aristotle's name; movements of the soul caused by external forces amounting to Fate; climate, natural endowments; Magna Moralia 1187b29 on phusis). Note: this is the sixth chapter of the Italian edition, omitted from the 2010 English translation (Prefatory Note), so Huby is the only witness in the fonds; 'Perhaps this was the line of thought' is Huby's hedge, preserved by the node. Printed p. 371 legible in the file (line 64); page_map unusable, page left empty.

### `scholarly_argument_donini_nicomachean_ethics_and_the_vol_4` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Nicomachean Ethics 3.5 and the voluntariness of virtue and vice — At NE 3.5 1113b7-14, Aristotle is concerned with whether virtue and vice are voluntary, producing arguments aimed at showing that they are indeed voluntary; Donini argues that this passage is inconsistent with the view found elsewhere in Aristotle that a virtuous man can act only virtuously (a claim the reviewer P. M. Huby rejects as misplaced, holding that Aristotle here merely aims to establish that virtue and vice are voluntary, without psychological qualification)
- Constat : Two problems. (2) Donini's own text (2010 tr., ch. V 'Character and indeterminacy', pp. 103-105) does not simply 'argue that this passage is inconsistent': he says NE III 5, 1113b7-14 'seems utterly incompatible with the theory of unidirectionality of habits' and then proposes two hypotheses to reconcile them, concluding that 'it is legitimate for Aristotle to say that everyone has the option of doing good or evil even after the acquisition of a habit'. Pages from running heads (103, 105) confirmed on PDF; page_map wrong (98), page left empty.
  - « It would be inappropriate at this point to add qualifications about human psychology such as D. wants. So his claim that this passage is inconsistent with the view found elsewhere that a virtuous man can act only virtuously is misplaced. » [aristotle-and-determinism-pier-luigi-donini-ethos-aristotele-e-il-determinismo-culture-antiche-studi-e-testi-2-pp-vi154-alessandria-edizioni-dellorso-1989-paper-l-30000.md:58-60]
  - « Aristotle could coherently state in NE that every agent has the option of deciding in favor of a bad or a virtuous action if this thesis meant something different in NE than it seems to do in EE. » [Pier Luigi Donini - Aristotle and Determinism (2010, Peeters) - libgen.li.md:4123-4125]
  - « but he could still lose that habit, so it is legitimate for Aristotle to say that everyone has the option of doing good or evil even after the acquisition of a habit that, in fact, constrains them to always decide in one way and one direction only. » [Pier Luigi Donini - Aristotle and Determinism (2010, Peeters) - libgen.li.md:4160-4163]
- Description proposée : Donini (Ethos ch. V; Engl. tr. 2010, pp. 103-105) finds that NE III 5, 1113b7-14, where agents 'always have two possible options', 'seems utterly incompatible with the theory of unidirectionality of habits'. Rather than leaving the inconsistency, he offers two reconciling hypotheses (the original indeterminacy of the not-yet-formed character; a habit is not 'an absolutely irreversible condition'), concluding that 'it is legitimate for Aristotle to say that everyone has the option of doing good or evil even after the acquisition of a habit'. Huby (CR 1991, pp. 370-371) objects that Aristotle here merely shows virtue and vice are voluntary and that Donini's psychological qualifications are 'misplaced'.

### `scholarly_argument_koch_aristotle_s_alleged_doctrine_o_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Aristotle's alleged doctrine of fate — Aristotle developed no doctrine of fate and never used the term εἱμαρμένη in his transmitted writings except once in Poetics with a non-technical meaning; Alexander's claim to expound 'Aristotle's doctrine concerning fate' is a constructive project with minimal textual basis
- Constat : Quote verbatim at p. 36 (conf 0.95). Two qualifications lost: (1) Koch attributes the observation that Aristotle elaborated no doctrine of fate and never uses the term to 'les lecteurs et traducteurs modernes du traité' who 'ont tous souligné' it — a reported consensus, not her own discovery; (2) the single Poetics occurrence is characterised not as 'non-technical meaning' but as 'pas employé pour lui-même par Aristote': it belongs to Aristotle's summary of a tragic plot (the lost Phineidai), pp. 36-37. The constructive-project point is faithful.
  - « Le premier point que je développerai ici touche précisément à ce travail de construction, qu’Alexandre entreprend sur la base d’un donné aristotélicien quasi inexistant et dans un contexte scolaire jusqu’à lui peu développé. » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:152-154] (p. 36)
  - « les lecteurs et traducteurs modernes du traité ont tous souligné que non seulement Aristote n’avait élaboré aucune doctrine du destin, mais qu’on ne pouvait même relever aucun emploi du terme εἱμαρμένη dans tous ses écrits transmis. » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:156-159] (p. 36)
  - « Une unique occurrence s’en trouve dans la Poétique, mais le terme n’y est pas employé pour lui‑même par Aristote : il s’agit d’un passage où Aristote résume un épisode dramatique des Phinéides, tragédie que nous ne connaissons que par cette citation » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:160,192-194] (pp. 36-37)
- Description proposée : Koch (pp. 36-37) stresses Alexander's 'travail de construction' undertaken 'sur la base d'un donné aristotélicien quasi inexistant'. Facing the De fato's announced aim of expounding Aristotle's doctrine on fate, modern readers and translators 'ont tous souligné' that Aristotle elaborated no doctrine of fate and that the term εἱμαρμένη occurs nowhere in his transmitted writings, except once in the Poetics, where 'le terme n'y est pas employé pour lui-même par Aristote': it comes from his summary of an episode of the lost tragedy Phineidai.
- Pages : 36-37

  - « tient sans doute, au moins en partie, à deux faits : l’importance et le prestige que l’astrologie revêt à l’époque d’Alexandre, phénomène culturel, à dimension à la fois religieuse, politique et scientifique9 ; et la confrontation obstinée d’Alexandre aux thèses des stoïciens » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:146-150] (p. 36)

### `scholarly_argument_koch_middle_platonic_conditional_fa_5` — **MISATTRIBUTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Middle Platonic conditional fate theory — Middle Platonists developed a theory of conditional fate (destin conditionnel) from rereading foundational Platonic texts: our actions are free, but consequences of actions are subject to fate and inevitable; Alexander likely knew this theory but suppressed reference to avoid advertising a rival school in a work celebrating his accession to head of Aristotelian school
- Constat : The first half is Koch's (p. 35 and n. 7 for the summary 'nos actions sont libres, mais … les conséquences … sont soumises au destin et inévitables'). The second half — Alexander probably knew the theory but omitted it so as not to advertise a rival school in a book celebrating his accession — is Sharples's hypothesis (On Fate, p. 14), explicitly introduced by 'Selon Sharples' in n. 8 (p. 36). The node presents it as Koch's own position.
  - « C’est ainsi que les Platoniciens, sur les questions du destin et de la providence, ont actualisé leur platonisme en opérant une relecture de quelques textes fondateurs5 pour développer la théorie originale du destin conditionnel6 » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:112-115] (p. 35)
  - « On peut résumer cette doctrine du destin conditionné comme suit : nos actions sont libres, mais, une fois que nous avons agi, les conséquences de nos actions sont soumises au destin et inévitables » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:138-140] (p. 35, n. 7)
  - « Selon Sharples, Alexandre connaissait vraisemblablement la théorie médioplato‑ nicienne du destin conditionnel, mais il n’y fait aucune allusion dans son De fato, peut‑être pour ne pas faire de publicité à une école rivale dans un livre célébrant son accès à la tête de l’école aristotélicienne (On Fate, op. cit., p. 14). » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:162-165] (p. 36, n. 8)
- Description proposée : Koch (p. 35): the Platonists 'ont actualisé leur platonisme' by rereading founding texts to develop 'la théorie originale du destin conditionnel', found in Alcinous Did. 26, Calcidius In Tim. 142-144, Nemesius Nat. hom. 38 and ps.-Plutarch De fato; she sums it up (n. 7): our actions are free, but once done their consequences are 'soumises au destin et inévitables'. The suggestion that Alexander probably knew this theory but omitted it 'pour ne pas faire de publicité à une école rivale' is Sharples's (On Fate, p. 14), reported by Koch in n. 8 (p. 36).
- Pages : 35-36

### `scholarly_argument_koch_peripatetic_definitions_of_fat_1` — **MISATTRIBUTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Peripatetic definitions of fate and providence — The Peripatetic definitions of providence and fate in Alexander's two treatises are very similar and address identical concerns; Alexander restricts fate to the sphere of τέλος pursued by efficient, antecedent, natural causes, which brings his conception of fate very close to providence
- Constat : The first clause is Koch's (p. 34; also abstract p. 33). The second clause — Alexander restricts fate to the sphere of the τέλος pursued by efficient, antecedent, natural causes, bringing fate close to providence — is a quotation from Jaap Mansfeld ('Diaphonia', Phronesis 33, 1988, p. 195), translated by Koch and introduced by 'comme le note Jaap Mansfeld' (n. 3 gives the English original). The node attributes Mansfeld's observation to Koch.
  - « la définition péripatéticienne de la providence et celle du destin dans l’un et l’autre traités sont très proches et cherchent visiblement à répondre à des préoccupations identiques. La proximité est même si grande qu’on pourrait se demander en quoi les deux concepts diffèrent, comme le note Jaap Mansfeld : » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:58-62] (p. 34)
  - « On pourrait souligner que la restriction, opérée par Alexandre, de la portée du destin (dans le De fato) à la sphère du τέλος qui doit être poursuivi par les causes efficientes, antécédentes et naturelles, rapproche grandement sa conception du destin du concept de providence.3 » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:65-69] (p. 34, quoting Mansfeld)
  - « 3. Ibid., p. 195 : «So Alexander at least tried to keep the twins apart, although, as we shall see, there is some inevitable overlapping. » [Le_destin_et_la_providence_Sur_deux_trai.md:89-90] (p. 34, n. 3)
- Description proposée : Koch (p. 34) holds that the Peripatetic definitions of providence and of fate in the two treatises 'sont très proches et cherchent visiblement à répondre à des préoccupations identiques', so close 'qu'on pourrait se demander en quoi les deux concepts diffèrent'. The observation that Alexander's restriction of fate 'à la sphère du τέλος qui doit être poursuivi par les causes efficientes, antécédentes et naturelles' brings his conception of fate 'grandement' close to providence is Jaap Mansfeld's ('Diaphonia', Phronesis 33, 1988, p. 195), quoted by Koch (n. 3).
- Pages : 34

### `scholarly_argument_pr_aux_fate_and_free_will_in_minucius_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : fate and free will in Minucius Felix — Minucius Felix's Octavius contains a sophisticated philosophical treatment of fate (fatum) and divine providence that has been curiously neglected by scholars; the work addresses the tension between determinism and Christian doctrine through a dialogue format
- Constat : Quote verbatim (lines 42-49, printed p. 395). But what Préaux calls 'curieusement négligé' is a specific argument (Caecilius' fatalist argument, Oct. XI, 5-6, and Octavius' refutation, XXXVI, 1-2) and, on p. 398, the search for its sources — not a 'sophisticated philosophical treatment'. Préaux stresses the opposite: the apologist 'n'a pas tiré de son fonds' his answers, which he only 'esquisse', and Octavius himself calls it brief ('Ac de fato satis, uel si pauca pro tempore'). 'Determinism' is not Préaux's word (he writes fatum, 'fatalisme philosophique'). No page_map; pages 395-398 from running heads; page left empty.
  - « Il s'agit de l'argument emprunté à la doctrine du fatalisme philosophique qu'avance le païen Caecilius au chapi- tre xi, 5-6, et de la réfutation que lui apporte le chrétien Octavius au chapitre xxxvi, 1-2. » [Preaux_1950_A_propos_de_FR.md:47-50]
  - « Il est étonnant que l'on n'ait guère recherché, à ma connais- sance, les sources de la pensée de Minucius Felix dans ces passages importants qui traitent un problème situé au cœur même de la théologie et de la philosophie. Il est clair, cependant, que l'apolo- giste habile n'a pas tiré de son fonds les données et les réponses qu'il esquisse » [Preaux_1950_A_propos_de_FR.md:208-213]
- Description proposée : Préaux (pp. 395-398) reconsiders an aspect 'jusqu'ici curieusement négligé' of the Octavius: the argument drawn from 'la doctrine du fatalisme philosophique' advanced by the pagan Caecilius (XI, 5-6) and Octavius' refutation (XXXVI, 1-2), set within the apologist's mainly philosophical demonstration of divine providence. He finds it surprising that the sources of these passages have hardly been sought, and insists that the apologist 'n'a pas tiré de son fonds' the answers he merely sketches ('esquisse'). His thesis is that the treatment is borrowed, not that it is sophisticated.

### `scholarly_argument_pr_aux_interpretation_of_genitura_in__5` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : interpretation of genitura in Octavius XXXVI, 2 — genitura should be understood as 'theme of geniture' (astrological birth chart) and is intentionally juxtaposed with ingenii natura to form a meaningful wordplay
- Constat : Préaux does translate genitura as 'thème de géniture' and speaks of an intentional 'jeu de mots' with ingenii natura (p. 397 n. 2, continued p. 398, lines 193, 241-242). But in the announced discussion (pp. 410-411, lines 872-896) he allows both senses of genitura — horoscope in astrological language, or 'la nature engendrée, la créature' — and says 'tous les deux conviennent'; the point of the wordplay is that punishment falls on the ingenii natura, read through Origen's thought. The node drops this qualification. The Greek equivalent given in the file is OCR-corrupt ('( yévsaiç )', line 873) and cannot be verified. No page_map; page left empty.
  - « (2) C'est le sens que j'adopte pour genitura rapproché intentionnellement » [Preaux_1950_A_propos_de_FR.md:193]
  - « de ingenii natura pour former un jeu de mots, lourd de sens si on le replace dans la pensée de Minucius Felix. Voir plus loin la discussion, pp. 410-411. » [Preaux_1950_A_propos_de_FR.md:241-242]
  - « Quel que soit le sens qu'on donne à ce terme technique dans ce contexte-ci - et tous les deux con- viennent - , ce n'est ni le thème de géniture ni la nature créée qui est l'objet d'un châtiment divin, mais bien, affirme Octavius, Yingenii natura » [Preaux_1950_A_propos_de_FR.md:893-896]
- Description proposée : In his translation of Oct. XXXVI, 2 Préaux renders genitura as 'thème de géniture' and notes (p. 397 n. 2) that it is 'rapproché intentionnellement de ingenii natura pour former un jeu de mots, lourd de sens'. In the discussion (pp. 410-411) he explains that genitura designated either a birth horoscope in astrological language or, more generally, 'la nature engendrée, la créature', and that here 'tous les deux conviennent': whichever sense is taken, what is punished is neither the horoscope nor created nature but the ingenii natura — a doctrine he traces to Origen.

### `scholarly_argument_pr_aux_minucius_felix_s_originality_o_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Minucius Felix's originality on fate — Minucius Felix was not original on the problem of fate and providence; his treatment was inspired by earlier Church Fathers, though the specific Latin source is difficult to identify before Augustine
- Constat : Quote verbatim (lines 219-228, printed p. 398). Two qualifications lost: Préaux speaks of 'la démonstration d'un docteur de l'Église' (singular), and the point about Latin writers is not that the source is 'difficult to identify' but that no Latin Doctor before Augustine treated the problem at all (Tertullian, Cyprian silent; Arnobius and Lactantius only touch it, p. 399), so that the source must be Greek — which he identifies as Origen (p. 401: 'Ce procédé d'exposition a été emprunté, à mon sens, à Origène'; p. 402: Caecilius 'imite à la lettre l'exégèse origéniste'). No page_map; pages from running heads; page left empty.
  - « lui a été inspirée par la démonstration d'un docteur de l'Église. Il est impossible d'admettre que Minucius Felix ait fait preuve d'originalité sur ce point aussi capital que délicat à traiter. » [Preaux_1950_A_propos_de_FR.md:225-228]
  - « Avant saint Augustin, personne parmi les Docteurs de l'Église, écrivant en latin, n'a traité ce » [Preaux_1950_A_propos_de_FR.md:237-238]
  - « Ce procédé d'exposition a été emprunté, à mon sens, à Origène et c'est à la lumière des démonstrations célèbres que le grand penseur d'Alexandrie a fournies entre 220 et 250 que se révèlent les sources de la pensée de l'avocat romain » [Preaux_1950_A_propos_de_FR.md:377-380]
- Description proposée : Préaux (p. 398) holds it 'impossible d'admettre que Minucius Felix ait fait preuve d'originalité' on the fatum/providence problem: his characteristic way of resolving it 'lui a été inspirée par la démonstration d'un docteur de l'Église'. Since before Augustine no Latin Doctor treated the problem (Tertullian silent, Cyprian likewise; Arnobius and Lactantius merely touch it, p. 399), the source is Greek: the 'procédé d'exposition a été emprunté, à mon sens, à Origène' (p. 401), whose exegesis of Rom 9 Caecilius' argument 'imite à la lettre' (p. 402).

### `scholarly_argument_sorabji_epictetus_on_freedom_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)

  - « Not only does Epictetus regard this freedom as compatible with compulsion; he views it as so obviously compatible with the Stoic belief in determinism, that is, with the idea that whatever happens has all along been necessary or inevitable,4 that he does not even mention deterministic necessity throughout his discourse on freedom. » [Seaford, Richard_ Wilkins, John_ Wright, Matthew Ephraim (eds.) - Selfhood and the soul _ essays on ancient thought and literature in honour of Christopher Gill-Oxford University Press (2017).md:2161-2166]
  - « (iii) It is true that Epictetus does have the idea of prohairesis being free, but this important, but rarely achieved freedom, is compatible with things having all along been inevitable and with every attempt at duress. It is very different from the freedom of voluntas invoked before Epictetus by Lucretius and Cicero » [Seaford, Richard_ Wilkins, John_ Wright, Matthew Ephraim (eds.) - Selfhood and the soul _ essays on ancient thought and literature in honour of Christopher Gill-Oxford University Press (2017).md:2767-2771]
- Description proposée : For Sorabji (pp. 49-50) Epictetus' eleutheria is 'a genuine freedom, but rare': through 'careful adjudication of desires' one is 'enslaved to nothing', and the resulting 'rare kind of invulnerability' is why the person is called free. Epictetus regards this freedom as compatible with compulsion and with Stoic determinism. Against Frede, Sorabji does not present it as an incomplete free will but as a freedom 'very different' from the voluntas libera of Lucretius and Cicero and from Christian free will (p. 63), even though Justin, Tatian and Origen borrowed Epictetus' terminology for 'a completely different idea of freedom' (p. 64).

  - « An important change here is that the concept of freedom has been connected by Epicurus unmistakably to that of accountability or liability to blame, whereas in Plato the two concepts may have been used to make different points. » [Seaford, Richard_ Wilkins, John_ Wright, Matthew Ephraim (eds.) - Selfhood and the soul _ essays on ancient thought and literature in honour of Christopher Gill-Oxford University Press (2017).md:2256-2259]

### `scholarly_argument_sorabji_lucretius_on_voluntas_and_the__3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Lucretius on voluntas and the swerve — Lucretius attributes voluntas to the animus within the anima, a capacity made possible against the decrees of fate by the swerve of atoms; this defends free will against emerging Stoic determinism
- Constat : Quote verbatim (pp. 52-53). The node's 'defends free will' skips two explicit qualifications: Sorabji hesitates to translate voluntas as 'will' because it 'is not a rational enough desire, since it is found in horses', and notes that Lucretius opposes free willing 'not to necessitation itself, but to a Fate or Fates … that operate from indeﬁnitely far back'. The link to the Stoic view that things have been 'fated for the whole of time' is faithful. page_map unusable; pages from PDF running heads; left empty.
  - « I hesitate to translate voluntas as ‘will’ not for this reason, but because it is not a rational enough desire, since it is found in horses (2. 261–93). He opposes free willing not to necessitation itself, but to a Fate or Fates (fatum, fata) that operate from indeﬁnitely far back » [Seaford, Richard_ Wilkins, John_ Wright, Matthew Ephraim (eds.) - Selfhood and the soul _ essays on ancient thought and literature in honour of Christopher Gill-Oxford University Press (2017).md:2289-2293]
  - « The reference in Lucretius and Cicero to necessity from inﬁnite or everlasting time reﬂects the rise of the opposing Stoic view that things have indeed been fated for the whole of time. » [Seaford, Richard_ Wilkins, John_ Wright, Matthew Ephraim (eds.) - Selfhood and the soul _ essays on ancient thought and literature in honour of Christopher Gill-Oxford University Press (2017).md:2316-2318]
- Description proposée : Sorabji (pp. 52-53): Lucretius 'bases freedom on a swerve of the atoms'; voluntas 'is not an agent in its own right, but a capacity or act of animus', the central part, seated in the heart, of the physical soul anima. Sorabji hesitates to translate voluntas as 'will', since 'it is not a rational enough desire' (it is found in horses), and stresses that Lucretius opposes free willing 'not to necessitation itself, but to a Fate or Fates … that operate from indefinitely far back'; the appeal to necessity from infinite time 'reflects the rise of the opposing Stoic view that things have indeed been fated for the whole of time'.

The description drops Amand's hedge 'presque toutes les attaques' (almost all) and turns it into 'attacks ... were in reality attacks on the fatalism'. It also omits the framing sentence: the work is 'purement historique' and not a 'machine de guerre contre l'astrologie elle-même'. Page range X-XI: the passage is entirely on p. XI. agentic_search cannot project the front matter (p.?, map_conf=0.00, AUDIT-FAIL); page read from the running header.

- Description proposée : Amand: throughout Greek and Christian antiquity astrology was 'essentiellement et radicalement fataliste'; the theory of heimarmene and that of 'universal sympathy' formed its doctrinal basis, and absolute fatalism was a postulate taught and admitted by all astrologers. Consequently almost all ('presque toutes') the attacks on astrology by Hellenic philosophers and Christian theologians were in reality aimed at the fatalism bound up with the science of the 'Chaldeans'. He stresses that his work is purely historical and no war-machine against astrology itself.
- Pages : XI

### `scholarly_argument_amand_de_mendieta_carneades_anti_fatalist_moral__0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Carneades' anti-fatalist moral argumentation — Carneades developed (or at least sharpened and popularized) a moral argumentation against Stoic fatalism that survived and was transmitted through later Greek philosophers and Christian theologians of the first four centuries; this argumentation was based on the disastrous moral consequences of the doctrine of εἱμαρμένη (heimarmene/fate)

The central thesis of Dom David Amand de Mendieta (1945). Amand maintains that the anti-fatalist moral argumentation developed by Carneades against Stoic and astrological fatalism was preserved — often without it
- Constat : Stance and quote_verbatim are verbatim (Avant-propos p. IX, l. 213-219; the OCR garbles one word as 'ῥογία' where the sense requires 'porta'). The Conclusion material is verified: six 'textes témoins' listed pp. 571-572 (Philo, Alexander, Firmicus, Eusebius, Chrysostom, ps-Chrysostom; secondary witnesses Bardesane, Basile, the Arian commentator on Job, Némésios), the 3-of-6 rule p. 573, the six argument headings pp. 573-581, the p. 572 caveat. BUT the appended 'conjectural line of transmission Carneades → Clitomachus → Cicero → Philo → Alexander → Firmicus → Eusebius → Chrysostom' is not Amand's: he treats the witnesses as parallel users of a Neo-Academic source and reconstructs by synoptic comparison; for Methodius he explicitly excludes direct use through Clitomachus (l. 17714-17715). Page range VII-X is wrong: the Avant-propos begins on p. IX (X at l. 233, XI at l. 276).

- Description proposée : Amand: the moral argumentation drawn from the disastrous moral consequences of the doctrine of heimarmene is the one Carneades 'sinon inventée, du moins aiguisée et popularisée' against Stoic dogmatism; the book studies its survival among later Greek philosophers and Greek Christian theologians of the first four centuries. Six 'textes témoins' (Philo, Alexander, Firmicus Maternus, Eusebius, Chrysostom, ps-Chrysostom), plus Bardesane, Basil, the Arian commentator on Job and Nemesius, ground a conjectural reconstruction by synoptic comparison ('règle de fer': Carneadean if attested by 3 of 6). Amand proposes no linear chain of transmission between the witnesses.

- Description proposée : Amand never intended to write a history of the anti-fatalist polemic; the title 'Fatalisme et liberté' should not mislead readers seeking a synthetic, philosophically conceived exposition. His 'méthode strictement philologique et historique' obliged him to present a primarily documentary study and to stay behind texts clear enough by themselves; the same historical method led him to set the philosophers and Christian doctors in their concrete intellectual milieu, sketch their personality, note the Christians' attitude to Hellenic philosophy and how each conceived 'la liberté du vouloir'.

### `scholarly_argument_djeranian_chrysippus_s_compatibilism_0` — **MISATTRIBUTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Chrysippus's compatibilism (D'Jeranian) — Chrysippus's attempt to reconcile fate and moral responsibility through the cylinder analogy and the distinction between antecedent cause and principal cause ultimately fails; his strategy succeeds only against the anti-fatalist objection but not for grounding moral responsibility properly speaking
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim at l. 266-270, p. 5 of the author's version (agentic_search: p.5, map_conf 0.95; the file is a 17-page author manuscript, not the journal pagination). The second clause — 'succeeds only against the anti-fatalist objection but not for grounding moral responsibility properly speaking' — is Salles's conclusion (2005, pp. 47-49), 'suivie par Gourinat', reported in n. 12 (l. 123-124); D'Jeranian's own formulation is that the strategy guarantees 'a minima l'autonomie causale de l'homme'. Page range 1-2 should be 2, 5.
  - « Ainsi rapportée, la stratégie de Chrysippe garantit a minima l’autonomie causale de l’homme au sein du monde déterminé, mais semble malgré tout contredire, par la rupture qu’elle implique dans la séquence causale représentation-assentiment, la soumission de l’assentiment au destin. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Responsabilite_morale_et_destin_une_solu.md:266-269]
  - « R. Salles (2005, p. 47-49) conclut que la stratégie de Chrysippe réussit dans le cadre de l’objection anti-fataliste uniquement (mais pas pour fonder la responsabilité morale). Opinion suivie par Gourinat 2005, p. 272-273. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Responsabilite_morale_et_destin_une_solu.md:123-124]
  - « Mais elles obtinrent des résultats en demi-teinte, et ne légitimèrent plus tant la responsabilité morale que l’autonomie causale de l’homme dans l’économie du destin » [01_Philosophie_antique/Responsabilite_morale_et_destin_une_solu.md:93-95]
- Description proposée : D'Jeranian: as reported by Cicero (De fato 41-43), Chrysippus's strategy (cylinder analogy; fate as antecedent and auxiliary cause, assent as principal cause) guarantees 'a minima' man's causal autonomy within the determined world, yet seems to contradict the subjection of assent to fate; since assent cannot be both autonomous and fated, Chrysippean 'compatibilisme' 'aboutirait finalement à un échec'. That the strategy succeeds only against the anti-fatalist objection but not in grounding moral responsibility is Salles's conclusion (2005), followed by Gourinat, which D'Jeranian reports in a note.
- Pages : 2, 5 (author's version)

### `scholarly_argument_djeranian_cicero_s_critique_of_chrysippu_2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Cicero's critique of Chrysippus, as read by Olivier D'Jeranian — Cicero demonstrates that Chrysippus's strategy of making assent autonomous while keeping it preceded by external representation fails: assent cannot be both autonomous and determined, because the representation cannot be both cause and condition of autonomous movement; Chrysippus is forced to withdraw assent from fate, thereby abandoning his fatalist position and confirming (despite himself) the necessity of fate
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim (l. 81-91), p. 2 of the author's version. The description overstates and muddles: D'Jeranian writes that the strategy 'ne satisfait pas Cicéron, qui juge que...' and uses the conditional throughout ('aurait dû', 'contraindrait'), not 'Cicero demonstrates'. The clause 'abandoning his fatalist position and confirming (despite himself) the necessity of fate' contradicts the text: the dilemma is that Chrysippus should have explicitly withdrawn assent from external antecedent causes (joining the anti-fatalists, Fat. 44) 'au risque de confirmer la position nécessitariste' otherwise; there is no 'confirms the necessity of fate'.
  - « Toutefois, cette stratégie ne satisfait pas Cicéron, qui juge que l’autonomie de l’individu, pour que les évaluations morales et juridiques puissent être légitimes, exige que l’assentiment ne soit pas l’effet de causes externes antécédentes. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Responsabilite_morale_et_destin_une_solu.md:81-83]
  - « C’est d’ailleurs ce que Chrysippe, convaincu de la liberté des mouvements de l’âme (Fat. 39), aurait dû explicitement dire au risque de confirmer la position nécessitariste. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Responsabilite_morale_et_destin_une_solu.md:83-85]
  - « contraindrait donc Chrysippe à rallier le camp des anti-fatalistes en soustrayant explicitement l’assentiment au destin (Fat. 44). L’assentiment ne pourra donc être à la fois autonome et déterminé, parce que la représentation qui le précède ne peut en être à la fois la cause et la condition de son mouvement autonome (Fat. 34-36). » [01_Philosophie_antique/Responsabilite_morale_et_destin_une_solu.md:87-91]
- Description proposée : D'Jeranian's reading of Cicero: the strategy does not satisfy Cicero, who judges that legitimate moral and juridical evaluations require that assent not be the effect of antecedent external causes. Chrysippus, convinced of the freedom of the soul's movements (Fat. 39), should have said so explicitly, at the risk otherwise of confirming the necessitarian position; the break introduced in the representation-assent sequence would thus force him to join the anti-fatalist camp by explicitly withdrawing assent from fate (Fat. 44). Assent cannot be both autonomous and determined, since the preceding representation cannot be both cause and condition of its autonomous movement (Fat. 34-36).
- Pages : 2 (author's version)
