### `scholarly_argument_djeranian_the_anti_fatalist_objection_an_4` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : The anti-fatalist objection and its requirements — The anti-fatalist objection, as reported by Cicero (De fato 40) and Aulus Gellius (VII, 2, 5), holds that moral and juridical evaluations require the indeterminacy of human actions with respect to external causal factors; for moral and juridical evaluations to be legitimate, the agent's assent must not be the effect of antecedent external causes
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim (l. 42-44), p. 1 of the author's version; the second half of the description (assent must not be the effect of antecedent external causes) is Cicero's requirement as stated on p. 2 (l. 81-83) and repeated at l. 108-110 ('Pour préserver la responsabilité morale de l'homme, ses actions ne doivent pas être déterminées par des facteurs causaux externes').
  - « Son objectif était de traiter l’objection anti-fataliste, rapportée par Cicéron (De fato 40) et Aulu-Gelle (VII, 2, 5), selon laquelle les évaluations morales et juridiques requièrent l’indétermination des actions humaines eu égard à des facteurs causaux externes. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Responsabilite_morale_et_destin_une_solu.md:42-44]

### `scholarly_argument_djeranian_the_cylinder_analogy_and_its_l_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : The cylinder analogy and its limitations — Chrysippus's cylinder analogy, which makes fate the antecedent and auxiliary cause while the individual's nature and force is the principal cause, dissociates the necessity of fate and preserves individual autonomy; however, this rupture in the representation-assent sequence, designed to 'flee necessity' and legitimize moral evaluations, ultimately forces Chrysippus to join the anti-fatalist camp by explicitly withdrawing assent from fate
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim (l. 77-80), p. 2 of the author's version. Two qualifications lost: (1) the 'nature et force propre' belongs to the principal cause, which D'Jeranian identifies with assent itself, 'et non la partie directrice de l'âme' (p. 5, l. 256-257) — not 'the individual's nature and force'; (2) 'forces Chrysippus to join the anti-fatalist camp' is the upshot of Cicero's critique stated in the conditional ('contraindrait'), not a flat outcome.
  - « Dans le De fato (§§42-43), cette manœuvre dissocie la nécessité du destin et préserve l’autonomie de l’individu, en faisant du destin la cause antécédente et auxiliaire de l’action de ce dernier. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Responsabilite_morale_et_destin_une_solu.md:77-78]
  - « seulement que c’est l’assentiment, et non la partie directrice de l’âme, qui agit selon sa force et sa nature » [01_Philosophie_antique/Responsabilite_morale_et_destin_une_solu.md:256-257]
- Description proposée : D'Jeranian: in De fato 42-43 Chrysippus's manoeuvre dissociates the necessity of fate and preserves the individual's autonomy by making fate the antecedent and auxiliary cause of his action; man's movement is like a cylinder reacting to an external push in a way determined by the 'nature et force propre' of its principal cause — which is assent itself, not the ruling part of the soul. On Cicero's critique, the break this introduces in the representation-assent sequence, conceived to 'fuir la nécessité' and legitimise moral evaluations, would ('contraindrait') force Chrysippus into the anti-fatalist camp by withdrawing assent from fate (Fat. 44).
- Pages : 2, 5 (author's version)

### `scholarly_argument_gourinat_multiple_senses_of_prohairesis_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : multiple senses of prohairesis in Epictetus — prohairesis in Epictetus has three main senses: (1) a punctual decision, (2) the faculty of making decisions (also called 'faculté prohairetique'), and (3) in a more restricted sense, the preliminary choice of a way of life or character; Epictetus uses the term in both the preferential sense (from Aristotle) and the temporal sense (from early Stoicism)
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim in the résumé (l. 29-32), p. 93. The three senses are developed in section 2 'Les trois sens de la « prohairesis »' (l. 956ff, pp. 114-115); 'faculté « prohairétique »' is Gourinat's term (l. 568, p. 106); that Epictetus plays on both the preferential and the temporal sense is explicit (l. 957-961). Page range 96-97 covers only the two-senses preliminary; add 106, 114-115.
  - « Une analyse des différents sens de la prohairesis montre que cette notion apparaît à la fois comme une décision ponctuelle, comme la faculté de prendre des décisions, et, dans un sens plus particulier, comme le choix préliminaire d’un mode de vie ou d’un personnage. » [01_Philosophie_antique/La_prohairesis_chez_Epictete_decision_vo.md:29-32]
  - « Épictète semble en fait jouer sur les deux sens du terme, choisissant parfois le sens préférentiel (faculté s’exerçant ponctuellement sur des décisions ponctuelles) et plus rarement le sens de la ligne de conduite ini- tialement choisie » [01_Philosophie_antique/La_prohairesis_chez_Epictete_decision_vo.md:957-960]

### `scholarly_argument_koch_stoic_causal_theory_and_human__0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Stoic causal theory and human responsibility — Chrysippus developed specific causal distinctions (including auxiliary and proximate causes) within the framework of defending human responsibility against determinism, and these distinctions are best understood through Cicero's De fato as responses to Academic challenges
- Constat : Quote_verbatim located verbatim (l. 157-166), printed p. 90. The description inverts Koch's distinction: she says the causal distinctions become useful when it is a question of grounding human responsibility EITHER against the determinism attributed to the Stoics (the position attributed to Carneades) OR inside the doctrine that everything happens by fate (the position Chrysippus defends). Chrysippus grounds responsibility within fate; 'defending human responsibility against determinism' is Carneades' side. 'Auxiliary and proximate' is correct (pp. 99-100, l. 569-584: 'causes auxiliaires et prochaines (adiuvantes et proximae)'). Section I runs pp. 91-103.
  - « elles apparaissent lorsqu’il est question de fonder la responsabilité humaine, qu’il s’agisse de la fonder contre le déterminisme attribué aux Stoïciens (position notamment attribuée à Carnéade dans le traité) ou de la fonder à l’intérieur de la doctrine selon laquelle tout arrive par le destin (position défendue par Chrysippe). » [01_Philosophie_antique/Distinctions_causales_stoiciennes_et_aca-2.md:161-166]
  - « D’où l’intérêt du De fato de Cicéron, qui attribue sans ambiguïté des distinctions causales précises à Chrysippe, en indiquant en outre le contexte de leur formulation. » [01_Philosophie_antique/Distinctions_causales_stoiciennes_et_aca-2.md:157-159]
- Description proposée : Koch: Cicero's De fato is valuable because it attributes precise causal distinctions unambiguously to Chrysippus and indicates their context, which confirms that these distinctions become useful, and are then drawn, within the problematic of responsibility: when it is a question of grounding human responsibility, either against the determinism attributed to the Stoics (the position given to Carneades in the treatise) or inside the doctrine that everything happens by fate (Chrysippus's own position). Chrysippus's distinctions (causae perfectae et principales vs adiuvantes et proximae, De fato 41) thus serve to ground responsibility within fate, not against determinism.
- Pages : 87-91; 91-103 (section I)

### `scholarly_argument_l_hr_gnostic_determinism_as_heresio_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Gnostic determinism as heresiological cliché — The charge of Gnostic determinism originated as a heresiological polemical construct in Irenaeus, Clement, and Origen, rather than being an accurate description of actual Gnostic theological positions. Before assessing whether any particular Gnostic source was deterministic, one must understand the heresiological agenda that generated the charge.
- Constat : The quote_verbatim is in the file (ll. 22-27, p. 381) and the second sentence of the description is faithful. But the clause 'rather than being an accurate description of actual Gnostic theological positions' asserts what Löhr explicitly refuses to decide: he brackets 'the extremely complicated question whether the charge of Gnostic determinism is a just description' and only adopts a 'sceptical methodological approach'. His own thesis is genealogical: the cliché was 'inaugurated/created by Irenaeus, developed by Clement and perfected by Origen' (p. 386).
  - « Although we do not address ourselves to the extremely complicated question whether the charge of Gnostic determinism is a just description of the theological attitude of one particular Gnostic source or another, we assume that recent research on this question justifies our rather sceptical methodological approach » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:28-32] (Löhr 1992, p. 381)
  - « Our observations suggest that the cliché of Gnostic determinism is part of a distinct hereseological tradition that was inaugurated/created by Irenaeus, developed by Clement and perfected by Origen. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:212-214] (Löhr 1992, p. 386)
- Description proposée : Löhr studies the 'hereseological cliché' of Gnostic determinism as it appears in Irenaeus, Clement of Alexandria and Origen: a distinct heresiological tradition 'inaugurated/created by Irenaeus, developed by Clement and perfected by Origen'. He explicitly does not address whether the charge is a just description of any particular Gnostic source; he only argues, on a 'sceptical methodological approach', that before assessing the justice of the charge one must become aware of the heresiological attitude that generated it.
- Pages : 381, 386-387

### `scholarly_argument_l_hr_irenaeus_s_anti_determinist_ar_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Irenaeus's anti-determinist arguments and their philosophical sources — Irenaeus employed well-known philosophical anti-determinist arguments against the Valentinians, particularly the 'lazy argument' (ἀργὸς λόγος) originally used by the Sceptical Academy against Stoic determinism, and the argument that determinism removes moral responsibility (praise/blame). These were widespread in second-century philosophy (Alkinoos, Alexander of Aphrodisias) and theology (Justin Martyr).
- Constat : Both arguments are correctly located (lazy argument, haer. II,29, p. 382; praise/blame, haer. IV,37,2, p. 383) and the quote_verbatim matches ll. 99-108. Two slips: (a) Löhr says the praise/blame argument 'goes back at least as far as Aristotle', was 'probably' used by the Sceptic Academy and was widely used in the 2nd century (Alkinoos, Alexander, Justin) — the description transfers this diffusion to both arguments ('These were widespread'); (b) it drops Löhr's qualification that Irenaeus 'nowhere directly compares Valentinian determinism with Stoic determinism'. The Greek term in the description cannot be verified: the file's rendering is OCR-corrupt ([Greek-corrupt: '&py6q X6yoq']).
  - « For example already the sceptical Platonic Academy refuted with the same argument (the so called […] the 'lazy argument') the determinism of the Stoics. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:66-68] (Löhr 1992, p. 382)
  - « This argument goes back at least as far as Aristotle; it was probably used by the Sceptic Academy against the Stoics; it was widely used in the 2nd century by philosophers as, for example, Alkinoos or Alexander of Aphrodisias or theologians like Justin Martyr. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:105-108] (Löhr 1992, p. 383)
  - « But although the bishop of Lyon refutes determinism with anti-Stoic arguments, he nowhere directly compares Valentinian determinism with Stoic determinism. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:113-115] (Löhr 1992, p. 383)
- Description proposée : Löhr reads Irenaeus' critique of Valentinian eschatology (haer. II,29) as 'the Christian version' of the 'lazy argument' with which the sceptical Academy had refuted Stoic determinism, and haer. IV,37,2 as a second 'philosophical argument against Stoic determinism': the completely determined cannot be praised or blamed. This second argument 'goes back at least as far as Aristotle', was 'probably' used by the Sceptic Academy, and was widely used in the 2nd century by Alkinoos, Alexander of Aphrodisias and Justin. Yet Irenaeus 'nowhere directly compares Valentinian determinism with Stoic determinism'.
- Pages : 382-383

### `scholarly_argument_l_hr_clement_of_alexandria_s_adapta_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Clement of Alexandria's adaptation of heresiological determinism — Clement, despite having first-hand knowledge of Valentinian sources and understanding that 'saved by nature' referred to the pneumatic element, nevertheless adopted and intensified Irenaeus's heresiological picture. He discovered 'gnostic determinism' even in innocent-seeming fragments of Basilides and Isidorus, and described Gnostic determinism in Stoic terminology without making the Stoic-heresy connection explicit.
- Constat : quote_verbatim found verbatim at ll. 127-133 (p. 383). All elements of the description are in Löhr: first-hand knowledge of Valentinian sources, 'saved by nature' referring 'first and foremost to the pneumatic element of the soul', use of Irenaeus' heresiological picture against Basilides/Isidorus fragments, Stoic terminology without explicit Stoicism-heresy link (p. 384). 'Intensified' is a gloss but is covered by 'never tires to discover the ghost' and the conclusion 'developed ... into an hereseological cliché'. Minor: Löhr's n. 15 adds that Clement 'tried to strike a balance' between first-hand information and the inherited cliché.
  - « Clement of Alexandria had a first-hand knowledge of gnostic, especially Valentinian sources and he knew that the phrase 'saved by nature' […] does refer first and foremost to the pneumatic element of the soul. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:120-123] (Löhr 1992, p. 383)
  - « Clement describes the Gnostic determinism of Valentinus, Basilides and Isidorus in Stoic terminology; 11 but he does not make explicit the alleged connection between Stoicism and heresy. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:148-150] (Löhr 1992, p. 384)

### `scholarly_argument_l_hr_clement_s_use_of_stoic_concept_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Clement's use of Stoic concepts against determinism — Clement paradoxically employed Stoic philosophical concepts—particularly the faculty of assent (συγκατάθεσις/ἡγεμονικόν)—to combat what he perceived as Gnostic determinism, drawing on Chrysippus's distinction between internal and external causes. However, this was a weak argument because Basilides himself had used 'assent' for faith, and because a nature-based determinism could simply identify nature with the perfected faculty of rational assent.
- Constat : quote_verbatim matches ll. 167-181 (pp. 384-385). Three points: (a) 'paradoxically' is not Löhr's; he says 'it is interesting to observe'. (b) 'drawing on Chrysippus's distinction' overstates: Löhr says that by taking up the Stoic term Clement 'brings into play another facet' of the debate, then explains Chrysippus' distinction himself — he does not claim Clement drew on Chrysippus. (c) The two Greek terms in the description cannot be verified from the file: the Greek is dropped at ll. 154, 156, 161, 167 and OCR-corrupt at l. 171 ([Greek-corrupt: auYxaT&6Eavs]); Löhr's English is 'the soul's faculty of assent'. The assessment of weakness (Basilides' own use; nature = perfected faculty of assent) is faithful.
  - « By taking up the Stoic term Clement brings into play another facet of the ancient philosophical debate about determinism: Chrysippus had defended the Stoic position against its Academic critics by distinguishing between internal and external, primary and secondary antecedent causes: The primary cause is always to be located inside man; it is precisely the soul's faculty of assent. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:161-166] (Löhr 1992, p. 384)
  - « To be sure, Clement's employment of the Stoic term against Basilides and Valentinus was not a particularly strong and helpful argument. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:167-169] (Löhr 1992, p. 384)
- Description proposée : Löhr observes that Clement fights 'gnostic determinism' with philosophical concepts from several sources: the Aristotelian/Stoic concept of what is 'up to us' and the Stoic concept of 'the soul's faculty of assent', which 'guarantees its freedom and moral responsibility'. Taking up this Stoic term 'brings into play' Chrysippus' distinction between internal and external causes. Löhr judges the argument 'not a particularly strong and helpful' one: Basilides himself defined faith as an assent, and stressing assent 'was of no avail against a position that tried to argue with the nature of a person', since nature could be identified with the perfected faculty of rational assent.
- Pages : 384-385

### `scholarly_argument_l_hr_origen_s_expansion_of_the_dete_4` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Origen's expansion of the determinism charge to Marcion — Origen took up Clement's work at its weakest point and expanded the charge of Gnostic determinism to include Marcion alongside Valentinus and Basilides, forming a standard trio of determinist heretics. Origen developed Clement's emphasis on the soul's faculty of moral choice/assent, defending human freedom against both the extreme that denies all external influence and the extreme that blames inborn character.
- Constat : quote_verbatim matches ll. 182-197 (p. 385). But 'took up Clement's work at its weakest point' misreads 'at precisely that point where Clement had left it', which Löhr immediately glosses as the amalgam Basilides/Valentinians to which Origen adds Marcion — not a judgement about weakness. The description also omits Löhr's further step: Origen 'does not leave the problem there' and speculates that souls are responsible, through choices in a previous existence, for their present differences (p. 385).
  - « Origen seems to take up the problem of Gnostic determinism at precisely that point where Clement had left it. Whereas Clement had made an amalgam of Basilides and the Valentinian position by asserting that both claimed that some are saved by nature, Origen adds the third great heretic of the 2nd century, Marcion. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:182-186] (Löhr 1992, p. 385)
  - « But - as it is well known - Origen does not leave the problem there. […] Origen speculates about the possibility that the souls themselves are responsible for the differences in their present condition. These differences are not due to different natures, but they are accounted for by different moral choices in their previous existence. » [Alfried Löhr - 1992 - Gnostic Determinism Reconsidered.md:200-206] (Löhr 1992, p. 385)
- Description proposée : Löhr: Origen takes up Gnostic determinism 'at precisely that point where Clement had left it' — where Clement amalgamated Basilides and the Valentinians as teaching salvation by nature, Origen adds Marcion, so that the trio Valentinus/Basilides/Marcion recurs 'time and again'. In De principiis Origen develops Clement's suggestion of defending freedom by stressing 'the soul's faculty of moral choice or assent', against two extremes (freedom only without external influence; blame on inborn character). He 'does not leave the problem there': the souls' present differences stem from 'different moral choices in their previous existence'.

According to the scholar, once we have our two ingredients, will and freedom, we have encountered the recipe for the free will and this is Epictetus’s achievement. » [Plotinus Aristotle And TheOriginOfTheFreeWill-4213557.md:129-137] (Acosta López de Mesa 2012, pp. 34-35)

- Description proposée : Acosta López de Mesa reports Frede: the Stoic notion of freedom, established by Chrysippus as exousia autopragias ('the ability to act one's own'), and the notion of will (an 'ability of the mind or of reason to make choices') are first combined into a free will by Epictetus. She accepts this reading of the Stoics but argues that their 'moral intellectualism' cannot genuinely explain choosing evil: since there are no irrational desires in adults and all impressions are rational, 'we always chose the good even if we mistake it by evil and evil is a mere accident caused by our ignorance of the real good'.
- Pages : 34-36

### `scholarly_argument_acosta_l_pez_de_mesa_role_of_plotinus_in_evaluating_3` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : role of Plotinus in evaluating intellectualist free will — Plotinus's analysis will be used to evaluate the limitations of the intellectualist free will; this suggests Plotinus offers resources for a broader conception beyond mere rational choice
- Constat : The quoted abstract sentence is verbatim (l. 34-35, p. 32), but the inference 'Plotinus offers resources for a broader conception beyond mere rational choice' inverts the article. Plotinus is her 'exemplar' of the limits of the intellectualist tradition 'founded by Socrates and strengthened by the Stoics'; her conclusion is that in Plotinus 'the complexity of freedom is totally lost' and that his 'philosophy is a philosophy suited for the One, not for human beings'.
  - « Thus, in order to show my own perception of the limits of the Stoic point of view on a purely rational will, I shall analyze Plotinus as exemplar. » [Plotinus Aristotle And TheOriginOfTheFreeWill-4213557.md:190-192] (Acosta López de Mesa 2012, p. 36)
  - « Now, Plotinus is also follower of this moral intellectualist tradition founded by Socrates and strengthened by the Stoics. » [Plotinus Aristotle And TheOriginOfTheFreeWill-4213557.md:304-305] (Acosta López de Mesa 2012, p. 39)
  - « To conclude, in my opinion, Plotinus’ philosophy is a philosophy suited for the One, not for human beings. » [Plotinus Aristotle And TheOriginOfTheFreeWill-4213557.md:680-681] (Acosta López de Mesa 2012, p. 49)
- Description proposée : Acosta López de Mesa uses Plotinus as the 'exemplar' with which to show the limits of a 'purely rational will': he is 'also follower of this moral intellectualist tradition founded by Socrates and strengthened by the Stoics'. Since for him authentic freedom is self-determination in Intellect and evil is involuntary, 'the complexity of freedom is totally lost'; his philosophy 'is a philosophy suited for the One, not for human beings'. Plotinus thus illustrates the limitation, not a broader alternative — that alternative she finds in Aristotle.
- Pages : 32, 36, 39, 48-49

The label 'cosmic determinism' is the node's, not Dettwiler's.

Dans le cadre de cette conception du cosmos, Ep développe une christologie de la seigneurie universelle. » [Epître aux Ephésiens commentaire.md:71-74] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 846)

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_divine_election_and_predestina_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)

- Constat : The sentence on election/'prédestination' expressing 'une pure gratuité du choix de Dieu' is verbatim (ll. 202-207), but it lies on printed p. 850, not 849 (PDF folio at page foot; agentic_search map_conf 0.90). More seriously, 'this is not deterministic in the philosophical sense' has no basis in Dettwiler, who never raises philosophical determinism; the node imports the thesis's frame into the commentary.
  - « Les v. 4-6 soulignent l'élection des destinataires, dès avant la création, par le Dieu de Jésus Christ. Le langage de l'élection ou de la « prédestination » (v. 5), précédant tout agir humain, exprime l'idée d'une pure gratuité du choix de Dieu. » [Epître aux Ephésiens commentaire.md:202-207] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 850)

Vv. 7-10 then move from God's eternal project to its realisation in the historical action in Christ. Dettwiler says nothing about determinism.

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_human_moral_agency_and_ethical_1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : human moral agency and ethical responsibility — The letter develops an ethics of contrast emphasizing necessary distance from the surrounding world; believers are called to active ethical response (4:17-5:20) despite the prior divine initiative in election and salvation
- Constat : 'Éthique du contraste' and 'nécessaire prise de distance' are verbatim (ll. 91-94, p. 847; developed pp. 861-862). But 'despite the prior divine initiative' inverts Dettwiler's relation: the ethic is 'fondée sur une expérience religieuse préalable dont elle ne saurait être dissociée' (p. 861), an 'éthique de la reconnaissance' (p. 854), structured by the Pauline indicative/imperative scheme (p. 862). Divine action grounds the ethical call; it is not its obstacle.
  - « En fait, Ep insiste beaucoup sur une éthique du contraste (voir notamment 4,17-5,20]: elle accentue la nécessaire prise de distance des communautés pauliniennes à l'égard du monde ambiant » [Epître aux Ephésiens commentaire.md:91-93] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 847)
  - « Le schéma paulinien classique de la relation entre« indicatif »et« impératif» se retrouve en 5,8 » [Epître aux Ephésiens commentaire.md:758-759] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 862)
  - « La vie quotidienne concrète de la communauté est le lieu de vérification de la nouvelle existence reçue par le Christ. » [Epître aux Ephésiens commentaire.md:738-740] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 862)
- Description proposée : Dettwiler: Ephesians insists on an 'éthique du contraste' (4,17-5,20), stressing the necessary distance of the Pauline communities from the surrounding world and warning against excessive acculturation. This ethic is grounded in, not opposed to, prior divine action: it is 'fondée sur une expérience religieuse préalable dont elle ne saurait être dissociée', an 'éthique de la reconnaissance', following the Pauline indicative/imperative scheme (5,8); daily community life is 'le lieu de vérification de la nouvelle existence reçue par le Christ'. The instructions appeal to each member's responsibility and discernment (5,10.17).
- Pages : 847, 861-862

### `scholarly_argument_dettwiler_theological_anthropology_from__2` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : theological anthropology: from death to life — The author emphasizes the dramatic contrast between human past marked by death and radically new present life in Christ (2:1-10), suggesting transformation of human condition through divine action rather than gradual moral improvement
- Constat : The 'contraste saisissant' between a past 'marqué par la mort' and 'leur vie présente en Christ, radicalement nouvelle' is verbatim (ll. 108-110, p. 847) and developed at p. 853, where God is 'acteur déterminant' and the transfer is 'un acte de résurrection spirituelle'. But 'rather than gradual moral improvement' is an antithesis Dettwiler never draws; he instead stresses that the addressees bore 'l'entière responsabilité' of their former sins while living a life 'aliénée par les puissances démoniaques'. Page 847 is correct for the introduction; the commentary proper is p. 853.
  - « D'abord, l'auteur rappelle aux destinataires le contraste saisissant entre leur passé, marqué par la mort, et leur vie présente en Christ, radicalement nouvelle (2,1-10]. » [Epître aux Ephésiens commentaire.md:108-110] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 847)
  - « Ils en portaient, certes, l'entière responsabilité [le texte parle« de vos fautes et des péchés » ], mais en même temps c'était une vie tragique, aliénée par les puissances démoniaques surnaturelles. » [Epître aux Ephésiens commentaire.md:351-354] (Dettwiler 2012, p. 853)
- Description proposée : Dettwiler: Eph 2,1-10 recalls to the addressees 'le contraste saisissant' between their past, marked by death, and their radically new present life in Christ. The former life was under the grip of death: they bore 'l'entière responsabilité' for their sins, yet it was 'une vie tragique, aliénée par les puissances démoniaques surnaturelles'. The accent falls on God as 'acteur déterminant', whose action is unconditional and creative love; the transfer from death to life is 'un acte de résurrection spirituelle' and a displacement 'dans les cieux', away from the demonic sphere.
- Pages : 847, 853

### `scholarly_argument_engberg_pedersen_pauline_pneuma_and_stoic_cosmo_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Pauline pneuma and Stoic cosmology — Paul's concept of pneuma ('spirit') is material and bodily, drawing on Greco-Roman philosophical cosmology, particularly Stoicism, rather than being purely immaterial or spiritual in a modern sense
- Constat : The material/bodily pneuma thesis is verbatim in the Preface (p. vii) and Introduction (pp. 3-4). But Engberg-Pedersen's contrast is not with a 'modern sense' of spirit: his explicit foil is 'immaterialistic and dualistic Platonism' / a 'Middle Platonic, immaterial understanding' as in Philo (pp. 4, 39), together with a purely 'apocalyptic' reading that dismisses ontology (p. 39). Page range 'Preface, 1-37' is loose; the theses are stated at vii, 3-4 and 39.
  - « We shall ﬁnd that Paul understood the pneuma as a through and through material, bodily phenomenon. » [Cosmology and Self in the Apostle Paul_ The Material Spirit -- Troels Engberg-Pedersen -- 2010 -- Oxford University Press -- 9780199558568 -- a23951d9645e1336d44ca71273751a5f -- Anna’s Archive.md:386-387] (Engberg-Pedersen 2010, p. 3)
  - « But we shall see that his basic, philosophical reference point was materialistic and monistic Stoicism—which indeed was importantly present in the two other writers, too—rather than immaterialistic and dualistic Platonism, which in Philo’s case, at least, had the upper hand. » [Cosmology and Self in the Apostle Paul_ The Material Spirit -- Troels Engberg-Pedersen -- 2010 -- Oxford University Press -- 9780199558568 -- a23951d9645e1336d44ca71273751a5f -- Anna’s Archive.md:394-397] (Engberg-Pedersen 2010, p. 4)
- Description proposée : Engberg-Pedersen argues that Paul understood the pneuma 'as a through and through material, bodily phenomenon', as in Stoicism, where 'a material, bodily notion of pneuma' is central. His basic philosophical reference point was 'materialistic and monistic Stoicism' rather than the 'immaterialistic and dualistic Platonism' that had the upper hand in Philo; a 'Middle Platonic, immaterial understanding does not fit the facts', nor should the pneuma be read in exclusively 'apocalyptic' terms that dispense with the question of its ontological character.
- Pages : vii, 3-4, 39

### `scholarly_argument_engberg_pedersen_paul_and_the_judaism_hellenism_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Paul and the Judaism/Hellenism divide — Paul's thought should not be forced into either purely Jewish or purely Hellenistic categories; instead, Paul operates beyond this divide, with his apocalyptic Jewish framework being expressible in Stoic philosophical terms
- Constat : quote_verbatim verbatim at ll. 1655-1671 (p. 37, agentic_search map_conf 0.98). The 'beyond the divide' framing is the author's (his 2001 volume, cited in the Preface). But 'his apocalyptic Jewish framework being expressible in Stoic philosophical terms' generalises what he states with care: the world-view 'is certainly a Jewish, apocalyptic one' of which 'parts ... are also spelled out' in Stoic cosmological terms; and at p. 37 the Stoic input sits alongside Philo-type wisdom speculation and 'apocalyptic' imagery in a 'frictionless fusion'.

  - « Apparently, however, there was no friction at all in Paul’s mind between these ostensibly different ‘thought worlds’. On the contrary, there appears to have been a frictionless fusion. » [Cosmology and Self in the Apostle Paul_ The Material Spirit -- Troels Engberg-Pedersen -- 2010 -- Oxford University Press -- 9780199558568 -- a23951d9645e1336d44ca71273751a5f -- Anna’s Archive.md:1669-1671] (Engberg-Pedersen 2010, p. 37)
- Description proposée : Building on Paul Beyond the Judaism/Hellenism Divide (2001), Engberg-Pedersen holds that Paul's world-view 'is certainly a Jewish, apocalyptic one', but that 'parts of it are also spelled out by Paul in terms of Greco-Roman philosophical cosmology, in particular that of Stoicism'. In 1 Corinthians 15 Paul argues 'in a manner that should be called distinctly philosophical' (Stoic cosmology, Aristotelian physics, ekpyrōsis) while also drawing on Philo-type wisdom speculation and 'apocalyptic' imagery; between these 'thought worlds' there was 'no friction at all' — 'a frictionless fusion'.
- Pages : vii, 37

### `scholarly_argument_king_augustine_s_early_philosophica_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Augustine's early philosophical influences on free will — Augustine's initial knowledge of philosophy was derived from Cicero and Seneca in an eclectic mix heavily influenced by Stoic doctrines, including technical aspects such as the mind accepting or rejecting impressions in sensory experience
- Constat : Located at pp. x-xi (in-file page headers 'Introduction / x', 'xi'; agentic_search refuses this AUDIT-FAIL file, map_conf 0.40). The description is accurate on Cicero/Seneca and the 'eclectic mix heavily influenced by Stoic doctrines', but it drops King's qualification that Augustine knew most of these doctrines 'in their non-technical popular forms' and only 'occasionally shows some knowledge of the more technical aspects', of which the accepting/rejecting of impressions (lib. arb. 3.25) is his one example.
  - « Most of these doctrines Augustine knew in their non-technical popular forms: the unity of the virtues, the rule of reason over the emotions, the identification of virtue and happiness, strength of character as a defense against the vicissitudes of fortune. Yet he occasionally shows some knowledge of the more technical » [[Peter_King]_Augustine_On_the_Free_Choice_of_the_(BookFi).md:183-188] (King 2010, p. x)
  - « Augustine speaks of the mind accepting or rejecting the impressions with which it is presented in the course of sensory experience – fundamental points of Stoic doctrine, which he handles correctly. » [[Peter_King]_Augustine_On_the_Free_Choice_of_the_(BookFi).md:192-195] (King 2010, p. xi)
- Description proposée : King: Augustine had no formal philosophical training and, distrusting sectarianism, pursued philosophy 'as a syncretistic amateur'. His initial knowledge came from the authors of his rhetorical education, 'Cicero and Seneca above all, in an eclectic mix heavily influenced by Stoic doctrines'. Most of these he knew 'in their non-technical popular forms' (unity of the virtues, rule of reason over the emotions, virtue as happiness, strength of character); only occasionally does he show more technical knowledge, e.g. the mind accepting or rejecting impressions in sensory experience (lib. arb. 3.25), which he handles correctly.
- Pages : x-xi

### `scholarly_argument_labarri_re_anachronism_of_self_in_ancient_2` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : anachronism of 'Self' in ancient philosophy — The use of 'Self' as a substantive category for studying ancient philosophy is problematic and anachronistic; there is no corresponding Greek or Latin vocabulary, and scholars often proceed as if discussing realia despite acknowledging this lexical gap. The French 'Soi' itself is an innovation of Pierre Coste's 1700 translation of Locke, designed to distinguish from Cartesian 'Moi' with its reflexive and subjective connotations
- Constat : Both quotes are verbatim (ll. 55-58 and 60-63) and all on printed p. 7 (first page; the running head '8' opens l. 83). Two nuances lost: (a) the 'reflexive and subjective' connotations belong to Self/Soi ('insistant sur ce qu'il y aurait en elle de « réflexif » et de « subjectif »'), whereas the description's syntax attaches them to the Cartesian 'Moi'; (b) Labarrière proceeds by questions ('qu'est-ce à dire exactement ?') and attributes the 'no corresponding vocabulary' remark to the customary caveats of the publications themselves, with scare quotes on « correspond ».
  - « Soi, employé substantivement, est une innovation du traducteur français de l’Essai sur l’entendement humain de Locke, Pierre Coste (1700), pour rendre Self et distinguer ainsi cette notion du Moi cartésien en insistant sur ce qu’il y aurait en elle de « réflexif » et de « subjectif » ? » [De "ce qui dépend de nous".md:55-59] (Labarrière 2009, p. 7)
  - « nombre d’entre elles, après les remarques d’usage sur le fait que rien dans le vocabulaire grec, voire latin, ne « correspond » à notre moderne « conceptualité », embrayent comme si l’on parlait d’une façon ou d’une autre de realia » [De "ce qui dépend de nous".md:60-63] (Labarrière 2009, p. 7)
- Description proposée : Labarrière opens by asking what Self/Soi can mean in ancient philosophy, recalling that 'Soi, employé substantivement' is an innovation of Locke's French translator Pierre Coste (1700), coined to render Self and to distinguish it from the Cartesian Moi by stressing what is 'réflexif' and 'subjectif' in it. He finds it astonishing that many recent publications, after the customary remark that nothing in Greek or Latin vocabulary 'corresponds' to our modern conceptuality, proceed 'comme si l'on parlait ... de realia'.
- Pages : 7

- Nœud actuel : eph' hemin (what depends on us) in Aristotle and Alexander of Aphrodisias — The concept of eph' hemin in Aristotle's Nicomachean Ethics III and in Alexander of Aphrodisias has been neglected in recent scholarship on the self in antiquity, which has focused disproportionately on Hellenistic and Roman Stoic sources rather than the Peripatetic tradition
- Constat : quote_verbatim verbatim at ll. 160-174 (p. 9; agentic_search map_conf 0.95). ### `scholarly_argument_labarri_re_to_eph_h_min_ce_qui_d_pend_de__0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : to eph' hēmin / ce qui dépend de nous — The article examines the Greek concept of 'to eph' hēmin' (what depends on us) and its translation history, particularly how Pierre Coste's French translation of Locke introduced 'Soi' as a substantive term with reflexive and subjective connotations distinct from Cartesian 'Moi'
- Constat : The Coste quote is verbatim (ll. 55-58, p. 7), but the description conflates two things: the article's topic (to eph' hēmin in Aristotle and Alexander, against the vogue of 'Self' studies) and the remark on Coste, which concerns the coinage of 'Soi' to render Locke's Self — not 'the translation history' of to eph' hēmin. Labarrière makes no link between Coste's 'Soi' and eph' hēmin.
  - « Self, ce terme est de plus en plus en usage en anglais et toujours aussi malaisé à traduire en français, surtout si l’on se tourne du côté des études en philosophie ancienne. » [De "ce qui dépend de nous".md:49-51] (Labarrière 2009, p. 7)
  - « afin de parvenir à une question : quoi du eph’ hemin chez Aristote et Alexandre d’Aphrodise ? Je n’en dis pas plus à ce point. » [De "ce qui dépend de nous".md:107-109] (Labarrière 2009, p. 8)
- Description proposée : Labarrière's article asks what the fashionable category Self/Soi can mean for ancient philosophy — 'Soi' as a substantive being Pierre Coste's 1700 coinage to render Locke's Self, distinguished from the Cartesian Moi by its 'réflexif' and 'subjectif' overtones — and, after a selective survey of recent 'Self' literature, arrives at his own question: 'quoi du eph' hemin chez Aristote et Alexandre d'Aphrodise ?' The Coste remark concerns the word Soi, not the translation of to eph' hēmin.
- Pages : 7-8

### `scholarly_argument_labarri_re_voluntary_and_involuntary_heko_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : voluntary and involuntary (hekousion/akousion) in Aristotle — Aristotle's analysis of hekousion/akousion in EN III has primarily juridical significance concerning the imputability of fault, but must be understood within the broader framework of arete (virtue/excellence) and action (praxis), not reduced to modern concepts of will
- Constat : quote_verbatim verbatim at ll. 222-226 (p. 10). But 'primarily juridical' hardens a hedged claim Labarrière attributes to Vernant ('non sans raison'): the analyses 'avaient aussi et peut-être surtout une portée juridique'. The section title is a question ('Ébauches de la volonté dans EN, III ?'); 'not reduced to modern concepts of will' is the node's inference. 'praxis' is not Labarrière's term here — he writes 'Soit celui de l'acte' (the Greek praxeis appears only inside the Bodéüs translation he quotes).
  - « « Jipé », comme nous l’appelions tous, commençait par rappeler, non sans raison, que les analyses d’Aristote sur ce que l’on appelle traditionnellement le « volontaire » et l’ « involontaire » (hekousion/akousion) avaient aussi et peut-être surtout une portée juridique. » [De "ce qui dépend de nous".md:200-210] (Labarrière 2009, p. 10)
  - « il n’en faudrait pas pour autant oublier le cadre plus large dans lequel elles s’inscrivent : l’arete, « vertu » ou « excellence ». Soit celui de l’acte. » [De "ce qui dépend de nous".md:224-226] (Labarrière 2009, p. 10)
- Description proposée : Labarrière recalls, with Vernant ('non sans raison'), that Aristotle's analyses of hekousion/akousion in EN III 'avaient aussi et peut-être surtout une portée juridique': what is at stake is the imputability of fault, and Aristotle himself says the definition is useful to legislators. Yet one must not forget 'le cadre plus large dans lequel elles s'inscrivent : l'arete, « vertu » ou « excellence ». Soit celui de l'acte' — knowing how to behave in each situation, which cannot result from mechanical application of rules. The section heading keeps the question open: 'Ébauches de la volonté dans EN, III ?'.
- Pages : 10

### `scholarly_argument_michon_akrasia_weakness_of_will_0` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : akrasia / weakness of will — Thomas Aquinas's analysis of incontinence should not be reduced to a simple reason-passion conflict as Davidson claims; Aquinas does employ the will in explaining why one conclusion is preferred to another
- Constat : Michon does say, against Davidson, that Thomas introduced the concept of will; but he immediately adds that this introduction adds no explanatory factor because for Thomas the will follows the judgment of reason, and that the struggle still opposes reason to passion. The node's clause 'Aquinas does employ the will in explaining why one conclusion is preferred to another' asserts exactly what Michon denies. Pages also wrong: Davidson's reproach is on p. 177 and Michon's reply on pp. 185-186, not 178-180.
  - « à homas, Davidson reproche de s’en être tenu, dans son analyse de l’incontinence, à un face-à-face de la raison et de la pas- sion, chacune procédant à son syllogisme pratique, et de n’avoir pas eu recours à la notion de volonté, qui aurait eu le mérite d’expliquer pourquoi une conclusion était préférée à l’autre. » [Je_ne_fais_pas_ce_que_je_veux_mais_je_f.md:31-35] (Michon 2009, p. 177)
  - « contrairement à ce que disait Davidson, par exemple, homas a bien introduit le concept de volonté : toute action volontaire est due à la volonté (voluntarium est a voluntate), et l’acte incontinent est volontaire » [Je_ne_fais_pas_ce_que_je_veux_mais_je_f.md:405-407] (Michon 2009, p. 186)
  - « Enin, l’introduction de la volonté n’ajoute pas de facteur explicatif supplémentaire, puisque homas rappelle qu’elle suit le jugement de la raison. L’incontinent choisit un acte, il le veut, et donc il le juge bon. » [Je_ne_fais_pas_ce_que_je_veux_mais_je_f.md:410-412] (Michon 2009, p. 186)
- Description proposée : Michon recalls Davidson's reproach that Thomas confined incontinence to a face-off of reason and passion without recourse to the notion of will (p. 177). Against Davidson he notes that Thomas did introduce the will (voluntarium est a voluntate; the incontinent act is voluntary and even chosen, though eligens rather than ex electione). But this introduction adds no explanatory factor, since for Thomas the will follows the judgment of reason; the struggle still opposes reason to passion, and incontinence is a succession of judgments and therefore of wills (pp. 185-186).
- Pages : 177, 185-186

### `scholarly_argument_michon_interpretation_of_paul_s_ego_i_1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : interpretation of Paul's ego in Romans 7 — Thomas Aquinas adopts a double reading of Paul's 'ego': (i) Paul speaks in the person of sinful man generally, and (ii) Paul speaks in his own person as a man established in grace; Thomas prefers the second hypothesis but admits the first throughout the passage
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 143-149 (p. 180; 'homas' = OCR ligature drop for 'Thomas'). The description matches: two remaining readings (ib) every sinner / (iia) Paul himself as redeemed; Thomas prefers the second but admits the first, proposing a double reading throughout. One nuance not in the node: Michon presents the two readings as Augustine's successive interpretations (83 Questions vs Contra Julianum), reorganised via Kretzmann's two distinctions (p. 179).
  - « restent donc les deux seules lectures (ib) – Paul parle de tout homme pécheur — et (iia) — il parle de lui-même comme racheté. homas préfère la seconde hypo- thèse, mais admet la possibilité de la première, proposant donc une double lecture tout au long du passage » [Je_ne_fais_pas_ce_que_je_veux_mais_je_f.md:143-146] (Michon 2009, p. 180)
  - « homas évoque les deux interprétations successives avancées par saint Augustin. Dans les 83 Questions diverses, Augustin comprenait que Paul parlait de l’homme pécheur (in peccato existentis) » [Je_ne_fais_pas_ce_que_je_veux_mais_je_f.md:121-123] (Michon 2009, p. 179)

### `scholarly_argument_michon_moral_responsibility_and_the_n_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : moral responsibility and the 'not I but sin' formula — Against Davidson's charge that Paul's attribution of action to 'sin dwelling in me' amounts to hypocrisy and refusal of responsibility, Michon proposes a reading that responds to this accusation; the 'not I but sin' is not a denial of responsibility but describes the divided self where reason (the true 'I') is distinct from fleshly inclination
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 28-37 (p. 177, not 178). The node's gloss ('not a denial of responsibility but describes the divided self where reason (the true I) is distinct from fleshly inclination') is an inference. Michon's actual reply follows Thomas: ego = reason, distinct from the flesh (p. 180); under 'not I but sin' Thomas does not conclude that man loses free will, yet admits that the sinner becomes sin's slave and 'loses the freedom of the free man' (p. 181); in reading (i) the evil act is attributed principally to concupiscence, reason being instrumental (p. 182); decisively, both readings express the doctrine of original sin and the fomes (p. 187). These qualifications are lost.
  - « homas note que le ego doit de toute façon renvoyer à ce qui est principal en l’homme (ou en Paul), à savoir la raison, qui se distingue de la chair et doit lui commander même si elle peut aussi lui céder. » [Je_ne_fais_pas_ce_que_je_veux_mais_je_f.md:150-152] (Michon 2009, p. 180)
  - « homas n’en tire pas la conclusion que l’homme perd son libre arbitre, mais il admet que, si c’est bien par l’intellect et la volonté que je reconnais la loi (consentio legi), quand j’agis contre la loi, ce n’est plus moi, mais le péché en moi, et je suis alors esclave du péché » [Je_ne_fais_pas_ce_que_je_veux_mais_je_f.md:215-219] (Michon 2009, p. 181)
  - « L’opération du mal fait par la raison vaincue par la concupiscence ne doit alors pas être attribuée principalement à la raison, ce que l’on entend ici par « homme », mais à sa concupiscence ou son vice » [Je_ne_fais_pas_ce_que_je_veux_mais_je_f.md:247-250] (Michon 2009, p. 182)
- Description proposée : Michon recalls Davidson's remark that Paul attributes his action to sin in him, which some read as hypocrisy and refusal of responsibility, and announces a reading of Paul that answers it (p. 177). Following Thomas: the ego is reason, distinct from the flesh which it should command though it may yield (p. 180). Under 'not I but sin' Thomas does not conclude that man loses free will, yet admits the sinner becomes sin's slave and loses 'la liberté de l'homme libre' (p. 181); in reading (i) the evil act is attributed principally to concupiscence, reason being instrumental (p. 182). Both readings express the doctrine of original sin and the fomes (p. 187).
- Pages : 177, 180-182, 187

### `scholarly_argument_michon_the_will_in_sinful_man_first_r_2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : the will in sinful man (first reading) — In the first reading (man in sin), the sinner's reason consents to fleshly suggestions, yielding to sin under the influence of prior sins; the sinner acts against conscience. ### `scholarly_argument_uster_problems_with_the_standard_int_3` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)

- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 165-170 (draft p. 4). Description reproduces Šuster's list of open questions about the pairing of causal characterisations. Same pagination caveat: the fonds holds the draft; '67-68' is not verifiable.
  - « But several questions immediately emerge: are complete (perfect) and principal (primary) always paired or not? How about auxiliary and proximate? Are there any further subdivisions? And how do necessity and fate qualify the nature of links in the causal web? » [Suster - 2021 - Chrysippus, Cylinder, Causation and Compatibilism.md:167-170] (draft p. 4)

### `scholarly_argument_uster_stoic_causal_determinism_and_i_1` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Stoic causal determinism and its relation to modern compatibilism — Modern interpreters suggest Chrysippus maintained universal causal determinism in a more or less modern sense; the fate principle should be understood as general causal determinism rather than fatalism, with every state and event necessitated by prior causes
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 25-33 (draft p. 1). Two qualifications are lost: (1) the phrase 'should be understood as general causal determinism rather than fatalism' is not Šuster's claim; the Frede quotation he cites explicitly declines to distinguish fatalism from causal determinism, and Šuster merely notes that 'fate' is out of vogue in modern usage; (2) Šuster disclaims expertise, defers to specialists, and adopts the general causal reading of the fate principle as a working assumption ('I will work with...').
  - « I claim no expertise on Stoicism, here and elsewhere I will defer to experts. I do not have much to say about the notorious “providential” dimension of Stoic determinism either. » [Suster - 2021 - Chrysippus, Cylinder, Causation and Compatibilism.md:33-35] (draft p. 1)
  - « I will work with the most general causal interpretation of the fate principle (Bobzien 1998, 10): “Everything happens in accordance with fate.” On this view, every state and event – including our actions and their psychology – is necessitated by prior causes (Salles 2005, xiii). » [Suster - 2021 - Chrysippus, Cylinder, Causation and Compatibilism.md:41-43] (draft p. 1)
- Description proposée : Šuster notes that terminology has changed ('fate' is out of vogue; causal or general determinism is the modern usage) and that modern interpreters credit Chrysippus with a universal causal determinism in a more or less modern sense; he quotes D. Frede declining to distinguish fatalism from causal determinism. Disclaiming expertise and deferring to specialists, he adopts as a working assumption the most general causal interpretation of the fate principle (Bobzien): every state and event, including our actions and their psychology, is necessitated by prior causes (Salles).
- Pages : draft p. 1 (published pagination unverifiable)

### `scholarly_argument_uster_the_structure_of_stoic_action__2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : The structure of Stoic action theory and moral responsibility — Moral responsibility requires assent, which is situated between impression and final reaction; the power of confirming or withholding assent is the locus of responsibility even within a deterministic framework
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 86-91 (draft p. 2). Šuster summarises the Stoic theory of action after Salles and states, with Bobzien, that the power of giving or withholding assent is situated between impression and final reaction, and that any attribution of moral responsibility presupposes assent. The clause 'the locus of responsibility even within a deterministic framework' anticipates the paper's conclusion; at this point Šuster poses it as the question to be answered.
  - « Crucially (Bobzien 1998, 240) – the power of either confirming the impression, i.e. giving assent or withholding such confirmation is situated between the impression and our final reaction. » [Suster - 2021 - Chrysippus, Cylinder, Causation and Compatibilism.md:90-92] (draft p. 2)
  - « Any attribution of moral responsibility for our actions presupposes assent and if all of the “motions” of our mind are fated, how could we be held responsible? » [Suster - 2021 - Chrysippus, Cylinder, Causation and Compatibilism.md:97-98] (draft p. 2)

### `scholarly_argument_xie_kant_s_metaphysical_division_o_3` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Kant's metaphysical division of two causalities — Kant's solution depends on grounding phenomenal causality in noumenal causality, but this grounding is problematic: if actions are causally determined by natural causes as effects of empirical character, it is unclear how they can simultaneously be free as effects of intelligible character
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 204-213 (p. 56). But the 'two problems' are addressed to Wood's answer, not directly to Kant; the 'how can they be free' question is Wood's own, quoted by Xie. Xie's specific diagnosis is that Kant's making the effects of intelligible character part of the effects of empirical character is unconvincing (developed in Section III), and that Wood's rescue via 'causal efficacy' leaves natural causation merely apparent, so Kant is at best a 'non-conflicting incompatibilist' (p. 57). These are lost.
  - « Here I believe that part of the problem is due to Kant for his making the effects of intelligible character part of the effects of empirical character is unconvincing. (I will discuss this issue in detail in Section III.) » [What Is Kant A Compatibilist Or An Incompatibilist? 2009.003.md:212-214] (Xie 2009, p. 56)
  - « If this is the case, then natural causation is still only “apparent” causation, not real cau- sation. So the difficulty Wood raises himself has not been overcome by him at all. » [What Is Kant A Compatibilist Or An Incompatibilist? 2009.003.md:231-233] (Xie 2009, p. 57)
  - « The best we can make him is a non-conflicting incompatibilist. » [What Is Kant A Compatibilist Or An Incompatibilist? 2009.003.md:237] (Xie 2009, p. 57)
- Description proposée : Xie accepts Wood's 'key point' that for Kant phenomenal causality is grounded in noumenal causality, which seemingly reduces natural causality to a red herring. He raises two problems for Wood's answer: if every human action conforms to empirical causality, how are free actions imputable; and viewing antecedent events from another standpoint as effects of freedom does not render them free. Xie locates part of the problem in Kant's unconvincing move of making the effects of intelligible character part of the effects of empirical character; Wood's 'causal efficacy' leaves natural causation merely apparent, so Kant is at best a 'non-conflicting incompatibilist' (pp. 56-57).
- Pages : 56-57

### `scholarly_argument_xie_kant_s_position_on_compatibili_0` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Kant's position on compatibilism vs incompatibilism — Neither 'compatibilist' nor 'incompatibilist' adequately describes Kant, but incompatibilist is the more accurate term; Kant's view is unique because he separates freedom and determinism into two different worlds (noumenal and phenomenal) yet attempts to reconcile them
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 25-28 (abstract, p. 53). The 'two different worlds ... yet attempts to reconcile' point is on p. 53 and n. 1. Xie uses 'compatibilist/incompatibilist' himself, so the vocabulary is his.
  - « Kant thinks freedom and determinism are two separate causalities belonging to two different worlds, namely, noumenal and phenomenal worlds, and following two different laws, which makes him an incompatibilist; » [What Is Kant A Compatibilist Or An Incompatibilist? 2009.003.md:39-41] (Xie 2009, p. 53)
  - « It may seem that by separ- ating freedom and determinism into two different worlds, Kant tries to make them compat- ible, however, by doing so, Kant must have recognized their incompatibility in the first place. » [What Is Kant A Compatibilist Or An Incompatibilist? 2009.003.md:52-55] (Xie 2009, p. 53 n. 1)

### `scholarly_argument_xie_success_of_kant_s_solution_to__1` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Success of Kant's solution to the free will problem — Kant's solution is not a success because making the effects of freedom part of the field of appearance renders his solution incoherent and ambiguous; however, his attempt is groundbreaking because he at least separated freedom from the dominance of determinism
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 28-30 (abstract, p. 53); the 'groundbreaking' clause is in the abstract (p. 53) and the Conclusion (p. 76). Note Xie's concession that Kant's solution 'can also be regarded as a success in the context of his philosophy as Wood assesses' and that a successful solution may not have been available in Kant's time (p. 76).
  - « Despite this, I argue that Kant’s attempt to solve the free will problem is groundbreaking because he at least has separated free- dom from the dominance of determinism. » [What Is Kant A Compatibilist Or An Incompatibilist? 2009.003.md:30-32] (Xie 2009, p. 53)
  - « Because of this, his solution can be regarded in general as a failure, even though it can also be regarded as a success “in the context of his philosophy” as Wood assesses. » [What Is Kant A Compatibilist Or An Incompatibilist? 2009.003.md:1268-1270] (Xie 2009, p. 76)

### `scholarly_argument_xie_wood_s_interpretation_of_kant__2` — **FAITHFUL** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Wood's interpretation of Kant as compatibilist — Wood's interpretation contains contradictions: Wood acknowledges Kant believes freedom is incompatible with natural causation (which makes Kant an incompatibilist), yet still labels Kant a compatibilist because he thinks Kant succeeded in reconciling them; this reconciliation is questionable
- Constat : quote_verbatim located verbatim at lines 151-155 (p. 55). Description matches Xie's section 1 'Wood's Interpretation and Its Problems' (heading p. 54).

The reason is that Wood believes Kant has succeeded in reconciling freedom and nature. » [What Is Kant A Compatibilist Or An Incompatibilist? 2009.003.md:151-155] (Xie 2009, p. 55)

### `scholarly_argument_sommers_compatibilist_folk_intuitions__1` — **DISTORTED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : compatibilist folk intuitions about determinism and free will — Nahmias et al.'s experimental results showing majority compatibilist responses cast doubt on claims that the folk are 'natural incompatibilists' and shift burden of proof toward incompatibilists; however, this general approach suffers from significant practical and philosophical difficulties
- Constat : The first half of the stance is NMNT's inference ('According to NMNT, these results cast doubt ... and should ... shift the burden of proof'), reported by Sommers on p. 201, not Sommers's position. Sommers's own view (pp. 205-206) is that the quoted incompatibilist passages are rhetorical, that no incompatibilist argument has the compatibility question as a premise, and that incompatibilists can claim to have discharged the burden with arguments. The node presents as Sommers's claim what he reports and then contests.
  - « According to NMNT, these results cast doubt on the claims of philosophers, such as Rob- ert Kane and Galen Strawson, that the folk are ‘natural incompatibilists’ and should, at the very least, shift the burden of proof towards the incompatibilist side. » [Philosophy Compass - 2010 - Sommers - Experimental Philosophy and Free Will.md:149-151] (Sommers 2010, p. 201)
  - « The role of these passages is largely rhetorical, to get readers into an incompatibilist mood. There is no incompatibilist argument which features a premise like ‘free will and moral responsibility are incompatible with determinism’ » [Philosophy Compass - 2010 - Sommers - Experimental Philosophy and Free Will.md:368-370] (Sommers 2010, p. 205)
  - « NMNT believe that their results shift the burden of proof to the incompatibilist. It seems, however, that incompatibilists can accept that they have this ‘argumentative bur- den’ but claim that they have discharged it with, well, arguments. » [Philosophy Compass - 2010 - Sommers - Experimental Philosophy and Free Will.md:385-387] (Sommers 2010, p. 205)
- Description proposée : Sommers reports that Nahmias, Morris, Nadelhoffer and Turner (NMNT) obtained largely compatibilist answers (76% free will, 83% blameworthy in the supercomputer scenario) and that, according to NMNT, these results cast doubt on Kane's and Strawson's claims that the folk are 'natural incompatibilists' and shift the burden of proof to incompatibilists (p. 201). Sommers objects: the cited incompatibilist passages are largely rhetorical, no incompatibilist argument has the compatibility question itself as a premise, and incompatibilists can claim to have discharged the burden with arguments such as the consequence argument (pp. 205-206).
- Pages : 201, 205-206

- Description proposée : Sommers holds that experimental work on the compatibility question has yielded important insights (doubts about the folk's pretheoretic incompatibilism, the role of emotion, effects of personality and of how determinism is described), but that its general approach faces serious practical worries (describing determinism and moral responsibility to subjects without triggering fatalism or begging questions) and a deeper philosophical problem: incompatibilist arguments appeal to intuitions about cases and narrower principles, not about the compatibility question the studies probe (pp. 199, 204-206).

Young's own words are 'divine timing and agency', 'God's cosmic plan is determinative' (p. 247), not 'deterministic/predestined'.

### `scholarly_argument_highfield_divine_action_creates_and_empowers_freedom` — **PAGE_WRONG** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : Highfield argues from an analogy among creation, incarnation, salvation, and providence that divine and human action are not competitors. Through Word and Spirit, God's unilateral creative act gives creatures their distinct being and powers, including freedom; grace empowers rather than overpowers Christ's human will and the believer's response; providence therefore works before, through, with, inside, around, and after human action. On this model, divine sovereignty causes, sustains, and directs human freedom to its end without doing it violence.
- Constat : Content faithful: Highfield's section 'Divine Action in Creation, Incarnation, and Salvation' compares those acts with providence; creation's unilateral act gives creatures real being 'including the power of freedom'; the Spirit enabled Jesus' human will, grace 'empowered him for genuine freedom' rather than overpowering it; prevenient grace enables free reception; providence works 'before, through, with, inside, around, and after'; divine freedom does not compete with human freedom. The page_range cites a local PDF index ('local PDF pp. 129-131'), which is not a printed page; the page map projects p. 129-131 area only at map_conf 0.50 (below 0.90), so no printed page can be reported. Cite by file:line.
  - « Now, let us allow the analogy of faith to inform our thinking about the way God acts in the world by comparing the acts of creation, incarnation, and salvation with that of providence. » [Four Views on Divine Providence, ed Dennis Jowers , Stanley N. Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:4136-4138] (Highfield 2011; printed page unverified, map_conf 0.50)
  - « He triumphed over sin, not because his human freedom was overpowered by divine grace, but because divine grace empowered him for genuine freedom. » [Four Views on Divine Providence, ed Dennis Jowers , Stanley N. Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:4153-4155] (Highfield 2011)
  - « God works before, through, with, inside, around, and after human working to accomplish his will. » [Four Views on Divine Providence, ed Dennis Jowers , Stanley N. Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:4185-4186] (Highfield 2011)
- Description proposée : Highfield lets the 'analogy of faith' compare creation, incarnation and salvation with providence. God's unilateral act of creation gives creatures real and distinct being 'including the power of freedom'; in the incarnation the Spirit enabled Jesus' human will to affirm the divine will, grace empowering rather than overpowering his freedom; in salvation prevenient grace enables the free reception of more grace. Providence is no exception: God works 'before, through, with, inside, around, and after human working', empowering and directing human freedom to its God-appointed end without doing it violence; divine freedom creates and supports human freedom and does not compete with it.

Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:4328-4331] (Highfield 2011; printed page unverified)

Natural free will is inadequate for that goal; without grace it cannot be reached. Freedom is both negative (release from sin and death as slavemasters) and positive (the future glory of God's children); hence 'God controls by liberating' could equally be 'God liberates by controlling'.

Granting that the dead in sin cannot love God without the Spirit differs from saying that with the Spirit they must; Scripture teaches the first and urges yielding rather than resisting the Spirit.

  - « Where did bondage come from? And does God control whatever it is that we and creation are in bondage to? If yes, it seems that at least one thing violates the thesis that God controls by liberating. If no, it seems that at least one thing violates the thesis that God controls everything. » [Four Views on Divine Providence, ed Dennis Jowers , Stanley N. Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:4930-4933] (Boyd 2011; printed page unverified)
  - « Yet it is not clear why God should need providentially to “overrule” and “defeat” anything if everything is already providentially controlled by God. » [Four Views on Divine Providence, ed Dennis Jowers , Stanley N. Gundry - (2011 , Zondervan).md:4945-4946] (Boyd 2011)
- Description proposée : Boyd finds a 'foundational incoherence' in Highfield's thesis: providence 'controls by liberating' while God also exercises 'comprehensive sovereignty'. If God controls by liberating while controlling everything, everything should be liberated, and nothing should need liberating; if God controls the bondage, the control is not liberating, if not, it is not comprehensive. The problem recurs when Highfield calls sin a master from which we need release, and when providence must 'overrule' and 'defeat' sinful intentions already under divine control. Boyd suggests the essay should be titled 'God Controls by Oppressing in Order to Liberate'.

### `scholarly_argument_munier_free_will_and_determinism_in_j_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and determinism in Justin Martyr — The work is a comprehensive study of Justin's Apology that addresses theological themes including human freedom and salvation, though free will is not the central focus of the monograph; Munier presents Justin's theology within its second-century apologetic context
- Constat : The node is a generic placeholder that reports no substantive claim. Munier in fact treats free will explicitly in ch. IX.4: moral responsibility and free will are closely interdependent in Justin as in most Apologists; free will is 'une pièce maîtresse de la doctrine morale paléochrétienne' rejecting Stoic determinism no less than gnostic predestination; Justin's arguments (moral order with sanctions, unpredictable behaviour, Scripture), always in an eschatological context; responsibility rooted in free will alone, no notion of original sin; ignorance as chief cause of failings (pp. 115-117, printed folios read in the file). Also pp. 35, 38, 53, 78 (structural analyses of Apol. I,43-44 and II,7). The page_range 'VII, IX-X, 95-151' is front matter plus the whole theology chapter. Page map for this file is AUDIT-FAIL; pages here are read from the printed folios in the extraction.

- Description proposée : Munier treats free will within ch. IX.4 'L'homme et son salut'. Moral responsibility and free will are closely interdependent in Justin, as in most Apologists; free will is a 'pièce maîtresse' of early Christian moral doctrine, rejecting Stoic determinism no less than gnostic predestination (pp. 115-116). Justin's arguments: the necessity of a moral order with sanctions, the unpredictable and contradictory behaviour of individuals, and above all Scripture (Dt 30,15.19; Is 1,16-20), always in an eschatological context (p. 116). Responsibility is rooted in free will alone, without recourse to original sin; ignorance is conceded as the main cause of human failings (p. 117).

This applies to Moses, David, Job, Jesus, Peter, James and Paul; even the 'historical Paul' is a reconstruction from the available evidence, and the volume does not aim to reconstruct him but to study his receptions.

### `scholarly_argument_van_kooten_free_will_and_moral_responsibi_0` — **OVERSIMPLIFIED** (confiance high)
- Nœud actuel : free will and moral responsibility in Pauline anthropology — The work focuses on the 'image of God' and assimilation to God (homoiōsis theōi) as central to Paul's anthropology, examining how these concepts relate to human transformation and ethical development rather than explicit free-will debates; the tripartite man schema (body-soul-spirit) is analyzed in contexts where divine likeness and moral formation intersect with questions of human agency
- Constat : The core of the description is right: the book is about the 'image of God', 'assimilation to God' (homoiōsis theōi) and the trichotomy of spirit, soul and body, and van Kooten does say the assimilation is 'ethical' in nature. Two losses. (1) 'analyzed in contexts where divine likeness and moral formation intersect with questions of human agency' is not van Kooten's framing: 'human agency' never occurs in the book (grep: 'agency' only for 'the agency of Moses'). His stated contexts are (a) the Corinthian sophistic movement — the two anthropologies 'become intertwined, with the sole purpose of offering an alternative to the outward, superficial, physiognomic anthropology of the sophists' [Kooten - 2008 - Paul's Anthropology in Context.md:444-447] — and (b) the ethnic tensions at Rome, answered by a 'universalist anthropology' climaxing in the metamorphosis of the mind in Rom 12.1-2 [Kooten - 2008 - Paul's Anthropology in Context.md:18815-18821, 18970-18986]. (2) The only free-will language in the book is confined to the 4 Ezra / Vision of Ezra theodicy discussion (pp. 14-15): the universalistic image of God 'is balanced with a particular understanding of human freedom and responsibility' [Kooten - 2008 - Paul's Anthropology in Context.md:1323-1324]; the description's 'rather than explicit free-will debates' is therefore correct but this locus should be named. Page range '1-469' is the PDF page count, not printed pages: the text runs XV-XXII (Introduction) and 1-392 (chapters), bibliography 393-412, indices 413-444. Verified_reference's '469 pp.' likewise reflects the PDF. NB the page_map projection for this file is systematically 4 pages too low in the body (line 18832, after the running folio '389', is projected p. 385; line 1324, before folio '15', is projected p. 10) and 3 too low in the roman front matter (Introduction opens XV, projected xii); pages below are read from the running folios in the file.
  - « In this book I wish to trace the development of Paul’s anthropology, against the background of both ancient Judaism and pagan Graeco-Roman philosophy. Within Paul’s anthropology, the notion of man as the image of God takes an important place. » [Kooten - 2008 - Paul's Anthropology in Context.md:360-362] (printed p. XV, folio read from running header; page_map projects xii)
  - « both anthropologies – that of the image of God and that of the trichotomic composition of man – become intertwined, with the sole purpose of offering an alternative to the outward, superﬁcial, physiognomic anthropology of the sophists. » [Kooten - 2008 - Paul's Anthropology in Context.md:444-447] (printed p. XVII)
  - « This is the outcome of man’s identiﬁcation with Christ. As in the Platonic doctrine of assimilation to God (see § 2.2 above), the nature of this assimilation is ethical. » [Kooten - 2008 - Paul's Anthropology in Context.md:18855-18857] (printed p. 389; page_map projects 385)
- Description proposée : Van Kooten traces the development of Paul's anthropology against ancient Judaism and Graeco-Roman philosophy: man as the 'image of God' (Gen 1.26-27), transformed by 'assimilation to God' — the Platonic doctrine (Theaetetus 176b; Eudorus, Alcinous) whose nature is 'ethical' — and the 'trichotomic' anthropology of spirit, soul and body (1 Thess 5.23), a Greek philosophical reading of Gen 2.7 shared with Philo and Josephus. The two intertwine in the Corinthian letters as an anti-sophistic alternative to outward performance, and in Romans as a 'universalist anthropology' overcoming ethnic tensions, climaxing in the 'metamorphosis' of the mind (Rom 12.1-2). Free will appears only in 4 Ezra's theodicy.
- Pages : XV-XVII (Introduction); 14-15 (4 Ezra: 'human freedom and responsibility'); 124-180 (homoiōsis theōi, Plato to Nicaea); 199-219 (Paul, image and likeness); 269-312 (trichotomy); 340-392 (Romans, renewal of the mind) — printed folios read from running headers, not from page_map
